- **A countersigning regime for certificates of succession, run from a second seat.** *(From باب 13.)*
  "حضرت بابا فرید الدین گنج شکر رحمۃ اللہ علیہ جب کسی کو خلافت نامہ عطا فرماتے اس شخص کو تاکید فرماتے کہ
  ہانسی جا کر آپ (شیخ جمال رحمۃ اللہ علیہ) سے مہر لگواؤ' اگر آپ مہر لگا دیتے تو اس کا خلافت نامہ مستند
  سمجھا جاتا اور اگر آپ مہر نہیں لگاتے تو حضرت بابا صاحب بھی قبول نہ فرماتے" — whenever he granted anyone
  a **خلافت نامہ** he enjoined that person to go to **ہانسی** and have it **sealed** by Shaikh Jamal; if he
  sealed it the certificate was reckoned **"مستند"**, authentic; if he did not, Baba Sahib too would not
  accept it, saying **"پارہ کردۂ جمال را نتوانیم دوخت"**, what Jamal has torn we cannot stitch (p. 84,
  folio 80, حاشیہ 4 = سیر الاولیاء فارسی ص 179). **A veto over succession documents vested in a
  subordinate seat.**
- **The regime failing in a single case, and being set aside by the granting shaykh.** *(From باب 12.)*
  Sabir reached Hansi, entered Jamal al-Din's **خانقاہ** still mounted on his **"چنڈول"**, palanquin, and
  dismounted only "[illegible] میں" — **the place is lost, and that is the point at issue in the quarrel**;
  after the prayer Sabir produced "اپنے پیرو مرشد کی عطا کی ہوئی شال", the shawl given by his pir, and
  asked for the seal; a lamp was called for, the wind blew it out, and Jamal al-Din said the certificate
  would be signed tomorrow (pp. 78–79, folios 74–75, حواشی 1–2 = سیر الاقطاب فارسی ص 180). After the lamp
  was relit, "حضرت جمال الدین ہانسوی نے آپ کا خلافت نامہ پھاڑ دیا۔ خلافت نامہ پر مہر نہیں لگائی" — **he
  tore the certificate up and did not seal it** (p. 79, folio 75). Sabir reported the matter at Ajodhan;
  Baba Farid said "پارہ کردہ جمال را فرید نتواں دوخت"; then "حضرت بابا صاحب نے آپ کو کلیر کی ولایت سپرد
  فرمائی' آپ کی مثال پر اپنے دستخط کئے اور اس مرتبہ آپ کو حضرت جمال الدین ہانسوی کے پاس جانے کی تاکید نہیں
  فرمائی" — he entrusted him with the **ولایت of کلیر**, **signed the مثال himself**, and this time did not
  require him to go to Jamal al-Din (pp. 79–80, folios 75–76). **A shrine's grant procedure visibly
  overridden by the granting shaykh.**
- **"ولایت" as a territorial assignment in his gift, three times in the range.** The **ولایت of دہلی** is
  entrusted, with the instruction to get the certificate sealed at Hansi first (p. 78, folio 74); the
  **ولایت of کلیر** replaces it (p. 79, folio 75); and Shams al-Din Turk is told at his leave-taking "کہ
  پانی پت میں مستقل سکونت اختیار کریں' کیوں کہ **پانی پت کی ولایت** ان کے سپرد کی گئی ہے" — to take
  permanent residence at **پانی پت** because the wilayat of Panipat has been entrusted to him (p. 92,
  folio 88). *(The Panipat grant is then quoted from the other side, as the grantee's own claim, at p. 94,
  folio 90 — see Cross-cutting material.)*
- **The regalia of succession he confers, itemised.** To **برہان الدین صوفی**, the minor son of Jamal
  al-Din Hansvi, he "ان کو خرقہ خلافت' مصلا اور عصا عنایت فرمایا" — gave the **khirqa of khilafat, the
  musalla and the asa** — and directed him to spend some time in the service of Nizam al-Din Auliya
  (p. 85, folio 81).
- **Langar distribution as an assigned office at Ajodhan, with a twelve-year term.** "حضرت بابا فرید الدین
  گنج شکر رحمۃ اللہ علیہ نے لنگر تقسیم کرنے کی خدمت آپ کے سپرد فرمائی" — he entrusted Ala al-Din Ahmad
  Sabir with the **service of distributing the langar**; "آپ نے 12 سال کے عرصہ میں کچھ نہیں کھایا", over
  **twelve years** he ate nothing of it; when Baba Farid learned this he said "علاؤ الدین احمد صابر ہیں",
  and from that day the title **"صابر"** stuck (p. 78, folio 74). **A named kitchen office, a named
  office-holder and a stated term.**
- **Food, langar and mendicancy at the shrine town, described in detail** (p. 63, folio 59): he always
  fasted and broke his fast "شربت کے ایک پیالے سے جس میں متقی[OCR?] ہوتی تھی", from one cup of sharbat,
  drinking a little and distributing the rest to those present; of **two روغنی روٹیاں** he ate one and
  broke the second into pieces for the company; when he first came to Ajodhan "آپ اور آپ کے متعلقین **پیلو
  اور ویلہ[OCR?]** پر گزارہ کرتے تھے", he and his dependants subsisted on **pilu** and **vela[OCR?]**;
  **"آپ کے مریدوں نے سالہا سال زنبیل گردان کی ہے"**, his murids carried the **begging bowl** round for
  years; "کھانے میں نمک نہیں ہوتا تھا", there was no salt in the food; "توکل کا یہ حال تھا کہ جو کچھ آتا
  وہ خرچ کر دیتے تھے", whatever came in was spent. **A shrine's founding economy: alms collected by murids,
  wild fruit, and nothing held back.**
- **A land grant refused and cash accepted for redistribution.** "سلطان ناصر الدین جب اوچہ و ملتان گیا تو
  آپ کی خدمت میں حاضر ہوا' واپسی پر اس نے چارہ[OCR?] گاؤں کا فرمان اور کچھ نقدی آپ کی خدمت میں بھیجی۔ **آپ
  نے گاؤں کی مثال واپس کر دی اور نقدی وصول فرما کر درویشوں پر خرچ کی**" — **سلطان ناصر الدین** visited him
  on his way to **اوچہ و ملتان**, Uch and Multan, and afterwards sent a **farman for چارہ[OCR?] villages**
  and some cash; **he returned the villages' deed (مثال) and accepted the cash, spending it on the
  dervishes** (note 16) (p. 61, folio 57). **A precise distinction between a permanent revenue endowment,
  refused, and a one-off cash offering, accepted and immediately spent. The number of villages cannot be
  read; the amount of the cash is (not stated); the sultan is given no dates and no dynasty.**
- **Marriage into the ruling house.** Balban "اس نے اپنی لڑکی بی بی نہرزہ[OCR?] کی شادی آپ سے کی", married
  his daughter to him (p. 61, folio 57), and she is named first among the four wives (p. 62, folio 58).
  **Kinship with the sultanate stated flatly, on the page after the refusal of its land.**
- **Transmission of the ism-e a'zam as lineage property.** Muin al-Din Chishti instructed Qutb Sahib:
  **"اسم اعظم جو خواجگان چشت میں سینہ بہ سینہ چلا آتا ہے' اسے تلقین کرو"** — teach him the Greatest Name
  which has come **breast to breast** among the Khwajgan of Chisht; "آپ پر علم لدنی کا انکشاف ہوا اور
  حجابات کے پردے اٹھ گئے" (p. 61, folio 57). Investiture followed: a **خلعت** from Gharib Nawaz, and the
  **دستار، مثال اور خلافت کے دیگر لوازمات** from Qutb Sahib (p. 61, folio 57).
- **Chilla as an institution of the cell.** Qutb Sahib to Gharib Nawaz: "مسعود … رہ گیا ہے' **وہ چلہ میں
  بیٹھا ہے**", Masud is left out, he is sitting in chilla; the two then went to his **حجرہ** (p. 60,
  folio 56) — with the **چلہ معکوس** (p. 60, folio 56) and the hujra near the Ghazni gate (p. 59,
  folio 55).
- **Sama' endorsed, with a reason.** "آپ کو سماع کا بہت شوق تھا" (p. 63, folio 59), and the saying **"سماع
  راحت دل ہے اور اہل محبت کو جنبش دینے والا ہے' جو بحر محبت میں شناوری کرتا ہے"** — sama' is the heart's
  ease and sets the people of love in motion (p. 65, folio 61). **The book records no objection to it in
  this entry** — the contrast with the saying against sama in باب 1 (p. 27, folio 23) is the range's one
  doctrinal disagreement between two mapped rows.
- **Prescribed aurad and waza'if, one of them for economic relief** (pp. 65–66, folios 61–62): Sura Baqara
  recited between the sunnats and farz of the dawn prayer for three days fulfils a purpose (note 26); Sura
  An'am recited **seventy times** fulfils a need (note 27); "لا الہ الا انت سبحانک انی کنت من الظالمین"
  frees one from grief and worry (note 28); and **"حسب ذیل دعا اکثر پڑھنے سے معاش میں تنگی دور ہوتی ہے"** —
  frequent recitation of the following dua removes **straitness in livelihood** — the text printing it in
  full: "بسم اللّٰہ الرحمٰن یا دائم العز والملک والبقاء یا ذو الجلال و الجود والفضل والعطاء یا و دود ذو
  العرش المجید یا فعال ما یرید" (note 29) (p. 66, folio 62). **A devotional remedy for poverty attached to
  the saint by name — directly usable for what a Pakpattan visitor comes for.**
- **Teachings** (pp. 64–65, folios 60–61): six kinds of **توبہ** — of heart and tongue, eye, ear, hand,
  foot, and nafs (note 21); on the dervish — "درویش جو اس دنیائے دنی کی رفعت و جاہ کا خواستگار ہو … درویش
  نہیں ہے' درویشوں کا نام بد نام کرنے والا ہے اور مرتد طریقت ہے' کیونکہ فقراء کو دنیا سے اعراض ہے", a
  dervish who seeks this base world's eminence is no dervish but one who defames dervishes and an apostate
  from the tariqa (note 22); **"محبت کے ساتھ سو مقام ہیں"**, love has a hundred stations, the first being
  patience under affliction from the Friend (note 23); the **کلاہ** may not be worn until one guards the
  four worlds of eye, ear, tongue, and hand and foot (note 24); and four questions "چار چیزوں کے متعلق سات
  سو پیران طریقت سے پوچھا گیا' سب نے ایک سا جواب دیا", put to **seven hundred pirs of the tariqa**, all of
  whom answered alike — the wisest is he who abandons sins, the most alert he whom nothing troubles, the
  richest the contented, the neediest he who abandons contentment (note 25). Among the aqwal: **"تصوف
  مولیٰ کی صفا دوستی[OCR?] کا نام ہے"** (p. 65, folio 61).
- **Tabarruk distributed at Ajodhan, carried by a qawwal between two seats.** Sabir dismissed **حسن قوال**,
  Hasan Qawwal, with a few **"گولر"**, gular figs; he brought them to Baba Sahib at Ajodhan, "حضرت بابا
  صاحب نے وہ گولر حاضرین کو تقسیم فرمائے", who distributed them to those present, and whoever ate "اس کی
  کثافت دور ہوئی' اس کو نور باطن حاصل ہوا" (pp. 82–83, folios 78–79).
- **A household woman's report of a disciple's renunciation, and its approval.** There was a **کنیز** in
  Jamal al-Din Hansvi's house, "وہ پاک دامن خاتون تھیں", whom Baba Farid addressed as **"مادر مومناں"**,
  mother of the believers; asked "مادر مومناں! جمال ماچہ می کند", she submitted that since meeting Baba
  Sahib "انہوں نے گاؤں' اساب' جائداد اور شغل خطابت کو بالکل ترک کر دیا ہے۔ فاقہ کشی اور محنت پر کمر باندھی
  ہے" — he had wholly abandoned **village, means, estate (جائداد) and the occupation of preaching (شغل
  خطابت)**, girding himself to starvation and labour; Baba Sahib was delighted and said "الحمدللہ' خوش می
  باشد" (pp. 84–85, folios 80–81, حاشیہ 5 = سیر الاولیاء فارسی ص 181).
- **He also appears as father-in-law and maternal kinsman of Sabir**: he married his daughter **"خدیجہ
  بیگم عرف شریفہ"** to him at his mother's insistence (p. 80, folio 76), and Sabir came to Ajodhan with
  his mother and lived with his **ماموں**, maternal uncle, Baba Farid (p. 78, folio 74).
- **He gives khilafat to Shams al-Din Turk but declares it insufficient**, telling him "حصول نعمت و کمال
  تو موقوف بر مرشد دیگر است" — your attainment of the gift and of perfection depends on another murshid —
  and sends him to **کلیر** to Ala al-Din Ahmad Sabir (p. 92, folio 88).

**Legends & Miracles**

- **A child who would not drink, and a fast kept on his account.** There was doubt about the Ramadan moon;
  a buzurg staying there was asked whether to fast and said a child would be born at the qazi's house —
  Farid's father's — and "اگر اس نے دودھ پیا تو روزہ نہیں رکھنا چاہئے"; that night he was born, did not
  drink, and the people fasted (pp. 56–57, folios 52–53).
- **Mud that became sugar — the first reason given for the title "گنج شکر".** Walking to his pir in Delhi
  through the mud, fasting for seven days, his foot slipped and a little mud entered his mouth; "وہ کیچڑ
  خداوند تعالیٰ کے حکم سے شکر ہو گئی", by God's command it became sugar, and his pir said "خداوند تعالیٰ
  نے تیرے وجود کو شکر بنایا' اور خداوند تعالیٰ تجھ کو ہمیشہ میٹھا رکھے گا" (note 6 → تاریخ فرشتہ فارسی
  جلد دوم ص 388) (p. 57, folio 53).
- **Sugar denied, sugar restored — the second reason.** "دوسری وجہ یہ ہے کہ", the second reason is that a
  merchant carrying sugar on bullocks denied it — "نمک ہے شکر نہیں ہے" — and found **salt** in every sack
  at his destination; he came seeking a prayer and was told **"شکر ہو جائے گی"**, it will become sugar, and
  it did (note 7 → اخبار الاخیار اردو ترجمہ ص 109) (p. 57, folio 53). **The book prints both reasons and
  chooses neither.**
- **A single pomegranate seed.** When **جلال الدین تبریزی** met him at **کہتوال**, one seed of the
  pomegranates he had brought was left on the ground; Farid put it in his turban and broke his fast with
  it, felt **انشراح**, expansion, and his pir told him "سب کچھ اسی ایک دانے میں تھا", everything was in
  that one seed (p. 60, folio 56). *(The same episode is told in باب 2 from the other side, with him called
  **"قاضی بچہ"**, Qazi Bacha, living in the **جامع مسجد** at کہتوال[OCR?]: he kept the fallen seed in his
  turban and broke his fast on it, "ان کی طبیعت میں انشراح اور دل میں روشنی پیدا ہوئی" (p. 35, folio 31,
  حاشیہ 7 = اخبار الاخیار اردو ترجمہ ص 87), and at Delhi his pir told him "مسعود! جو انار کا دانہ تیری
  قسمت کا تھا' تجھ کو مل گیا' خاطر جمع رکھو" (p. 36, folio 32, حاشیہ 8). **Chunk 002 recorded this under
  Other saints, not under the row** — see Doubts D1.)*
- **A voice from the unseen accepting him.** After Gharib Nawaz prayed "خدایا! ہمارے فرید کو قبول فرما اور
  اکمل درویش کے مرتبہ پر پہنچا", "غیب سے آواز آئی" — a voice came from the unseen: **"ہم نے فرید کو قبول
  کیا' وہ وحید عصر ہو گا"**, we have accepted Farid, he will be the unique one of the age (note 15 → "معین
  الہند"[OCR?] از ڈاکٹر ظہور الحسن شارب ص 98) (pp. 60–61, folios 56–57).
- **A prophecy in the form of a falcon.** Gharib Nawaz to Qutb Sahib: "قطب! بڑے شہباز کو دام میں لائے اس
  کا آشیانہ سدرۃ المنتہی ہو گا" (p. 61, folio 57).
- **Tidings of a throne, fulfilled.** It is said he gave **النمان[OCR?]** the **مژدہ** of crown and throne,
  "چنانچہ وہ بادشاہ ہوا", and accordingly he became king as **غیاث الدین بلبن** (pp. 61–62, folios 57–58).
- **Twenty years upright**, in his own words as the author quotes them (note 14 → راحت القلوب اردو ترجمہ
  ص 300) (p. 60, folio 56).
- **A dervish drowned after being refused a comb.** A dervish asked three times for the comb on the
  musalla and at last said "کنگھی مجھ کو دو' تمہارے واسطے برکت ہو گی"; the saint answered **"جاؤ' میرے حال
  میں دخل نہ دو' تجھ کو اور تیری برکت کو میں نے آب رواں میں ڈال دیا"** — go, do not meddle in my state; I
  have cast you and your blessing into running water (note 30 → تاریخ فرشتہ فارسی ص 385); **outside
  Ajodhan** the dervish bathed in the river, could not come out, "ڈوب کر مر گیا", drowned and died
  (note 31 → خیر المجالس اردو ترجمہ ص 155) (p. 66, folio 62).
- **Dates that became pure gold.** Six dervishes asked for travel provisions and were given the dates
  lying before him; they were displeased and made to throw them away, and as they looked saw "وہ خرمے زر
  خالص کے ہو گئے ہیں" (note 32 → راحت القلوب اردو ترجمہ ص 151) (p. 66, folio 62).
- **Magic survived and forgiven**: the son of **شہاب الدین ساحر** worked magic on him, "آپ نے اس کا قصور
  معاف کیا" (p. 60, folio 56).

**Significance**

- **The chapter's source base is the broadest in the range, and mixed in language.** **سیر الاولیاء
  (فارسی)** carries eight of the thirty-two notes (one shared with two other titles), **تاریخ فرشتہ** six,
  and the Urdu translations **راحت القلوب** and **اسرار الاولیاء** five each; also جواہر فریدی ص 119،
  انوار العارفین (فارسی) ص 292، روضۃ الاقطاب (فارسی) ص 59، اخبار الاخیار، سفینۃ الاولیاء، خیر المجالس and
  فوائد القوائد as printed (pp. 67–68, folios 63–64). **Two notes cite the book's own author**: "دلی کے
  (22) بائیس خواجہ از ڈاکٹر ظہور الحسن شارب ص 38" (note 10) and "معین الہند"[OCR?] از ڈاکٹر ظہور الحسن
  شارب ص 98 (note 15) (p. 67, folio 63).
- **The author stacks reports and does not adjudicate**: two birth years (notes 3–4), two death years
  (note 18), two explanations of "گنج شکر", two origins for the name Farid al-Din, a khalifa count as
  "بعض نے لکھا ہے", three of four attributed risalas as merely "بتائی جاتی ہیں" (pp. 56–63, folios 52–59).
  **There is no editorial voice correcting the text anywhere in the chapter.**
- **His sayings are presented as usable religious technology**: the اوراد و وظائف rubric turns the saint's
  teaching into numbered prescriptions with stated effects — a need fulfilled, grief removed, livelihood
  eased (pp. 65–66, folios 61–62).
- **Evaluation is formulaic rather than argued**: "آپ کی تعلیمات تصوف کا بیش بہا خزانہ ہیں", his teachings
  are a priceless treasury of tasawwuf (p. 64, folio 60).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **D1. حضرت بابا فرید الدین مسعود گنج شکر: the three chunk workers in this range disagree, and the
  disagreement is unreconciled.** **Chunk 003 maps him** to **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** because
  باب 8 states the town twice — "اجودھن میں جو آج کل پاک پٹن کے نام سے مشہور ہے" (p. 59, folio 55) and
  "مزار پر انوار پاک پٹن میں فیوض و برکات کا سرچشمہ ہے" (p. 62, folio 58) — and calls the identification
  "not in doubt". **Chunk 002 declined**, because its pages place him only at **کہتوال[OCR?]**, living in
  the **جامع مسجد** and known as "قاضی بچہ", with no shrine, no town of burial, no death year and no
  silsila by name (p. 35, folio 31). **Chunk 004 declined**, because its pages place him only at
  **اجودھن** — never at Pakpattan — and give him no mazar, no death year and no silsila (pp. 78, 79, 82,
  92; folios 74, 75, 78, 88). **All three positions are recorded; none is chosen here.** The consequence
  for the merge: if a human accepts the identification, the pomegranate episode and the "قاضی بچہ"
  nickname (pp. 35–36, folios 31–32) and the whole of chunk 004's Ajodhan material (pp. 78–92, folios
  74–88) belong to the row; if not, the Pak Pattan mazar statement stands alone.

- **D12. Baba Farid's death year conflicts with his archive row, and the book gives two of its own.** Row
  **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar**: died **1266**. Book: **"5 محرم الحرام 670ھ"** as the primary date
  and **"664ھ"** as a variant attributed to "بعض", with note 18 citing three works (p. 62, folio 58).
  **The range converts nothing to the common era. Record 670 AH, 664 AH and the row's 1266 side by side.**

- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the reign of "شہنشاہ اکبر", the date printed "بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ"; quoted source — the sentence carries حاشیہ 5 of باب 49, "انوار العارفین (فارسی) صفحہ 367", p. 245, folio 241): in باب 49, on حضرت شیخ جلال الدین تھانیسری, under the heading "اکبر کی باریابی": "شہنشاہ اکبر بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ کو مزار حکیم کی بغاوت کو فرد کرنے کی غرض سے پنجاب جاتے ہوئے تھانیسر میں رکا" — Punjab is the destination of an imperial campaign against a revolt; the stop, and the shaikh's خانقاہ, are at تھانیسر, which is not said to be in پنجاب. "مزار حکیم" and "فرد" are printed so and carry no flag; the sense calls for a person's name and a verb of suppressing, so check the page image before quoting (p. 243, folio 239).

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_pain_and_grace.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book calls him "Fariduddin Shakarganj of Pakpattan" and "Fariduddin Shakarganj, the Chishti saint
of Pakpattan"; the index prints "Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar 4, 12" (p. 302, folio 302). **No tomb, no
shrine, no ʿurs and no custodian at Pakpattan** — all **(not stated)**; the place is given as his seat
only.

- **Saint** — **Named twice, both times with the same death year and the same seat.** First as the
  western limit of the Chishti dispersal — "His disciples and later members of the order wandered
  through the whole of India, from the Panjab (Fariduddin Shakarganj of Pakpattan, d. 1265) to the
  Deccan (Gisudaraz of Gulbarga, d. 1422)" (p. 8, folio 4). Then as the possible origin of Panjabi
  mystical verse — "In the Panjab, popular mystical poetry may have emerged as early as in the days of
  Fariduddin Shakarganj, the Chishti saint of Pakpattan (d. 1265)" (p. 16, folio 12). **"d. 1265" is
  printed both times; the archive row gives 1266 — see Doubts. The archive row's `Chishti` is
  confirmed.**
- **Apparatus** — The place headword **"Pakpattan 12"** (p. 307, folio 307) shares p. 12 with his own
  entry, **but the index does not join them** — **(not stated)**. A biography is listed: "Nizami,
  Khaliq Ahmad, The Life and Times of Shaikh Farid Ganj-i Shakar, Aligarh 1955." (p. 293, folio 293).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

| row | the book prints | the archive row |
|---|---|---|
| `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` | "d. 1265" twice (pp. 8, 16; folios 4, 12) | 1266 |

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind and the Panjab", "the Panjab"; refers to: the conquests "first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani", and "the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj, the Chishti saint of Pakpattan (d. 1265)" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The countries which were conquered first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani, Sind and the Panjab, could likewise boast of a rich literary heritage. In the Panjab, popular mystical poetry may have emerged as early as in the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj"; "Numerous folk songs and ballads existed but were never committed to writing" (p. 16, folio 12).
- (book's word: "the province"; refers to: "the late 17th and the 18th century" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The golden age of Muslim Panjabi poetry is the late 17th and the 18th century, e.g., the time when the province was invaded one time after the other by Mahrattas, Sikhs, and, in between, by the liberating Muslim armies" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "a Panjabi"; refers to: "even today", present day of writing; author's argument): of Warith Shah's *Hir Ranjha* (composed "around 1794"): "even today a Panjabi will be deeply touched when this moving story is sung" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Nadir Shah's invasion, "before he reached Delhi in 1739" — as printed; book's historical prose): Nadir Shah "first conquered Sind and the Panjab before he reached Delhi in 1739"; Ahmad Shah Abdali's influence "on the scene in Sind is not as important as it was in the northern areas of present day Pakistan" (p. 30, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "1750" and after, the reign of Ahmad Shah of Delhi, to "1754" — as printed; book's historical prose): as the Mughal empire "had broken into pieces", "Sikh threatened the Panjab"; "Nadir Shah's successor, Ahmad Shah Durrani, invaded the Panjab several times, coming from his native Afghanistan" (p. 50, folio 48).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "rural provinces"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and "later" poets, period otherwise not stated; author's argument): similarities with "Panjabi mystical folk poetry, which reached its apex with Bullhe Shah during exactly the same period" are "conspicuous"; Siraiki "forms the transition to Panjabi; thus, the imagery of both, or rather all three, linguistic areas is very closely related, as is to be expected from the inhabitants of predominatly [OCR?] [predominantly] rural provinces" (p. 154, folio 154).
- (book's word: "both Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "Sohni is one of the favorite folk tales in both Sind and the Panjab" (p. 156, folio 156).
- (book's word: "the poetry of Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "A topic which is not rarely found in the poetry of Sind and the Panjab is the spinning of cotton, one of the most important occupations of women"; the Kapa'iti [printed "Kdpd ?it?" [OCR?]] melodies are "traditional folk melodies sung by the womenfolk while spinning" (p. 160, folio 160).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "Panjabi contemporaries"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime (the book dates him "1689-1752"); author's argument): Latif "stands before us as the representative of that type of the rural mystical tradition in which his contemporaries in the Panjab and, to a certain extent, in the Pashto speaking areas were rooted"; "Shah Abdul Latif, as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267).

## From Christopher Shackle, Bulleh Shah: Sufi Lyrics

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/shackle_bulleh_shah_sufi_lyrics.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint** — named once, as one of the Five Pirs, with his place: "the Five Pirs (panj pīr) are
  named as the legendary Khwaja Khizr, **Farid Shakarganj of Pakpattan**, Baha ud Din Zakariya of
  Multan, Jalal ud Din Makhdum Jahaniyan of Uch, and Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan." (p. 186,
  folio 161, n. 2 on 122). Date, order and tomb **(not stated)**.
- **Significance** — the Five Pirs "are believed collectively to possess special powers", and
  "Their miraculous interventions on behalf of Ranjha are a notable feature of Varis Shah's Hīr"
  (p. 186, folio 161, n. 2 on 122); invoked in the verse at p. 122 (folio 97) and p. 136 (folio
  111).
- *Bibliography* — Talib, Gurbachan Singh. 1974. *Baba Sheikh Farid Shakar Ganj*. New Delhi:
  National Book Trust (p. 204, folio 179). This is the only Farid item in the volume the book
  itself distinguishes by an epithet; three further Farid items are left unresolved — see Doubts.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Three Farid items are unresolved between two rows.** *Fifty Poems of Khawaja Farid* (Shackle
  1983, Multan), *The Message of Diwan-i-Farid* (Qaiser 2009), and "Punjabi Sufi Poetry from Farid
  to Farid" (Shackle 2011) (pp. 203–204, folios 178–179). Candidates: `mithankot-kot-mithan`
  (Khwaja Ghulam Farid) and `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`. The book prints "Khawaja Farid"
  without "Ghulam", and its own title "from Farid to Farid" shows it treats two Farids as distinct
  without saying which is which. **Left unmapped**; only Talib 1974, "Baba Sheikh Farid Shakar
  Ganj", is unambiguous and is mapped above. If a human resolves "Khawaja Farid",
  `mithankot-kot-mithan` gains three bibliography bullets.

- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: Bullhe Shah's lifetime — the book gives "Bullhe Shah (d. 1758)"; book's historical prose): "He lived at a time of great social upheaval in Panjab,1 as the central authority of the declining Mughal empire came under increasing challenge from armed rebellion by the Sikhs and from local notables establishing their auton-omy" [text-layer?] [autonomy]; the "1" fused to "Panjab," is an endnote marker (note 1: "See further Nijjar 1972, Alam 1986."), not a figure; "these unsettled conditions are occasionally re­flected [text-layer?] [reflected] in the poetry" (p. 8, folio vii).
- (book's word: "eigh­teenth-century Panjab" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century Panjab]; refers to: "early eigh­teenth-century" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century], as the book states; author's argument): "Bullhe Shah’s critique of the social upheavals in early eigh­teenth-century [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century] Panjab, which is so interesting to many modern readers, represents only a very small, if vividly expressed, part of his poetry" (p. 15, folio xiv).
- (book's word: "Panjab", "Panjabi society"; refers to: the time of kāfī 34, "The twelfth century of the Hijri era", which the note says "corresponds to the eighteenth century C.E. (1100 A.H. = 1688 C.E.)" — digits as printed; editor's note): the kāfī's allusive verses "appear to refer to a separation of Bullhe Shah from Shah Inayat due to the troubled conditions then prevailing in Panjab"; the note compares kāfī 4 "for the allusions to the disturbed state of contemporary Panjabi society" (pp. 165–166, folios 140–141).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the poet's own time, "The twelfth century" as the verse puts it — see the previous bullet for the note's reading; translation of the poet's verse, kāfī 34 — FLAG: verse register, kept because it characterises the region): "The twelfth century4 has come with gaping mouth. The door of doom and torment has opened, and Panjab is in a bad state. It has been struck by fear of the pit of hell." (the "4" fused to "century" is an endnote marker) (p. 52, folio 27).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: period not stated in the note; editor's note on kāfī 143, n. 35): a verse is "Taken to be an allusion to the Sikhs, whose guerilla horsemen ultimately destroyed the Mughal authority in Panjab" (p. 193, folio 168).
- (book's word: "eighteenth-century Panjab"; refers to: the eighteenth century, as the book states; editor's note on kāfī 86, n. 3): "The Ranghars were a tribe of Muslim Rajputs who achieved prominence in eighteenth-century Panjab, and who were proverbial for their arrogance" (p. 179, folio 154).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the Mughal period and beyond", following "The Muslim conquest of northern India" — no date given; book's historical prose): "The Muslim conquest of northern India extended this Persianate cultural world to Panjab, where Persian remained the dominant literary language of the ruling elite through the Mughal period and beyond. A strong Sufi presence was rapidly established with the arrival of charismatic fig­ures [text-layer?] [figures] associated with the main Sufi orders" (p. 11, folio x).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the Sufi poets "who lived at different times", and their collection "only in the late nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): "the lyrics of Bullhe Shah and the other Sufi poets, who lived at different times and in different areas of Panjab, were never systematically preserved by an or­ga­nized [text-layer?] [organized] faith community, and they were for the most part rec­orded [text-layer?] [recorded] and assembled in printed collections only in the late nineteenth century" (pp. 9–10, folios viii–ix).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s" and after, as the book states; book's historical prose): "Only with the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s did the literary culture of the Sufi tradition come to be overlaid by new patterns of modernity"; Persian "was quite rapidly replaced as the language of education, administra-tion [text-layer?] [administration], and elite literature by Urdu and Eng­lish [text-layer?] [English]"; "Publishers in Lahore" brought the older Sufi poetry "into the new print culture" (pp. 11–12, folios x–xi).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "twentieth-century" understandings and "the new middle class of the colonial period", as the book states; book's historical prose): "In Panjab the formulation of nationalist un­der­stand­ings [text-layer?] [understandings] was no simple pro­cess [text-layer?] [process], given the complexities created by the simultaneous currency of three closely related languages, Urdu, Hindi, and Panjabi, whose differences were exploited to sharpen the rival cultural identities of Muslims, Hindus, and Sikhs, respectively" (p. 12, folio xi).
- (book's word: "western Panjab"; refers to: the setting of the Hir–Ranjha legend, period not stated; book's prose on the poet's legendary material — the book itself marks the unit as "western"): "The greatest of these local legends is the story of Hir and Ranjha, which is set in the world of the Jat pastoral tribes of western Panjab" (p. 20, folio xix).

## From Taufiq Rafat, Bulleh Shah: A Selection

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rafat_bulleh_shah_selection.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

Named in chunks 001, 002 and 004, always as **"Baba Farid"** and once as bare **"Farid"**; the book
never writes "Fariduddin", "Ganjshakar" or "Pakpattan". **No tomb, place or order is given anywhere**
— (not stated).

- **Saint**
  - **Dates, and the book contradicts itself on the birth year.** "(1175-1265)" twice — "Baba Farid
    (1175-1265) wrote in slokes" (p. 25, folio not stated) and "There is a total blackout of record
    before Baba Farid (1175-1265) whose vocabularly [OCR?] [vocabulary] is quite sophisticated."
    (p. 29, folio not stated) — against "(1173-1265)" twice: "In the west, during this period, Baba
    Farid (1173-1265) was writing his slokes in this same tradition from an Islamic sufi base. It
    seemed a reaction of the underprivileged to the domination of caste." (p. 30, folio 10) and note
    18's "Baba Farid (1173-1265)" (p. 242, folio 222). **The book does not resolve it.** A third
    register gives only a century: "The 12th century poet Baba Farid, the first of the Punjabi
    language extant" (p. 47, folio 27). **All three registers give 1265 for the death; the archive
    row carries 1266** — see *Doubts*.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The transmission chain, told twice and in most detail in note 18.** "The 12th century poet Baba
    Farid, the first of the Punjabi language extant, would have been without trace had not the Sikh
    saint Guru Nanak taken his slokes from a descendant of the poet two centuries later. Farid is a
    poet of the Granth and has survived with the sacred book of the Sikh religion." (p. 47, folio
    27) — **"a descendant of the poet" is the only descendant-line reference in the book, and it
    carries no name, no seat and no date** (not stated). Note 18: "Asaf Khan has put together in this
    book the latest research on the provenance of the poetry of Baba Farid (1173-1265). Guru Nanak
    (1469-1538) collected the great first poet's work from his heirs and wrote it down in his
    note-books which also anthologised 15 other sufi and bbakti [OCR?] [bhakti] poets of India. Guru
    Arjun (1553-1606) put together the first copy of the Holy Granth out of these notebooks and thus
    secured Baba Farid from obscurity. Today he is the most important poet of the Granth, with more
    slokes than any other selected poet." (p. 242, folio 222). He is also listed among the Granth's
    contents at p. 41, folio 21.
- **Significance**
  - **The vocative-signature tradition is traced to him, not to Bulleh Shah.** "The tradition already
    existed in relation to Baba Farid and a number of Sikh gurus wrote poetry using 'Farida' as
    vocative." (p. 240, folio 220).
  - **One of Bulleh Shah's two unacknowledged models.** "Bulleh Shah does not mention either Rumi or
    Baba Farid, classics in whose technical and intellectual traditions he was steeped. Two of his
    Kafis are exact quotations from their work, 'Ke janan mein koan' and 'Neb Wi doongi' [OCR?] [Neh
    wi doongi]" (p. 37, folio 17).
  - **Form.** "Baba Farid's slokes are called kafis" (p. 49, folio 29); he is one of the three
    predecessors whose formal constraint sets off Bulleh Shah's freedom (p. 25, folio not stated) and
    one of the two who "had no personal reference to make to an actual preceptor-beloved" (p. 45,
    folio 25).
  - **Shared temperament with Bulleh Shah**: the confessional vein "so pronounced in Baba Farid and
    Bulleh Shah" (p. 30, folio 10).
  - **Preferred to Bulleh Shah by East Punjab's Sikh scholars** "in favour of Baba Farid and Hashim
    Shah, both integrally related to their religion and politics" (p. 51, folio 31).
  - **The edition cited, with publisher, city and year.** "Khan, Muhammad Asaf, Akbiya Baba Farid nay
    [OCR?] [Akhiya Baba Farid nay] (Pakistan Punjabi Adabi Board, Lahore 1978)." (p. 242, folio 222).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Silsila.** The archive gives `mazar-of-bulleh-shah` the silsila **Qadiri**; **Rafat supports it**
  ("the Qadiriya tradition to which his preceptor Shah Inayat belonged", p. 39, folio 19; "hard times
  for the Qadiriya order", p. 241, folio 221), **Khaled Ahmad does not** — he attaches Qadiriya only
  to Shah Enayat (p. 22) and "the Qadiriya custom of Sama" to a practice Bulleh Shah adopted (p. 24),
  which is not an affiliation statement, and the seventy-one poems supply nothing. The archive gives
  `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` the silsila **Qadri Shattari**; **the book never mentions the
  Shattari order at all.** The archive gives `madho-lal-hussain` **Malamati**; the book's "malamatia
  overtones" (p. 24) is the only support and it is a description of a tone, not an affiliation.
  `mausoleum-of-waris-shah` (Chishti), `garh-maharaja-shorkot` (Sarwari Qadiri),
  `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` (Chishti) and `mithankot-kot-mithan` (Chishti) get **no order at
  all** from this book.

- **Baba Farid's birth year: "(1175-1265)" twice (pp. 25, 29) against "(1173-1265)" twice (pp. 30,
  242).** Unresolved by the book.

- **Baba Farid: the book's death year is 1265 in all three of its date registers (pp. 25, 29, 30,
  242) against the archive's 1266;** and the loose "The 12th century poet Baba Farid" (p. 47,
  folio 27) is a century where the archive has a year.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's lifetime, "a time of great upheaval. The Mughal Empire was breaking up" — as the Translator's Note states it; Translator's Note, Taufiq Rafat): "the Punjab was being persistently rent by petty chiefs and marauders"; "The full bitterness of Bulleh Shah’s ‘Punjab has gone to the dogs’ will only come through against this backdrop" (p. 16, folio xvi).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's adult life, from Aurangzeb's death "in 1707" (margin heading "End of Mughal rule 1707.") — as the book states; book's historical prose, Introduction): Bulleh Shah's "depressive outlook was in no small measure produced by the political situation in the Punjab"; he was "deeply bruised" by the upheavals "taking place in the Punjab and was depressed by the sufferings of the common man"; "After Aurangzeb, the halcyon days that the Punjab had enjoyed quickly came to an end. Already badly defended, the North-Western region of the empire became increasingly subject to foreign" invasions "and inner centrifugal quarrels" (p. 26, folio 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the region", "the Pun-jabi of today"; refers to: from "The period of Muhammad Shah" (accession "in 1719" as printed) "into the 19th century", with a claim about the present day; author's argument, Introduction): that period "coincided with the beginning of the misfortunes of the Punjab. The decay of Mughal administration gave rise to small local tyrannies which lasted into the 19th century and formed the character of the" inhabitants "of the region"; the crisis of character in the Punjabi of today "is traceable to this period of duress in the history of his land" (pp. 26–27, folios 6–7; the sentence carries note 4 — see the Janus bullet below).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after "The invasion of Nadir Shah in 1738" and Abdali's invasions "in 1748 and then a third in 1752," — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "Marauding Sikh bands formed themselves into robber baronies and imposed their own system of extortion on the Punjab farmer"; Abdali's "treatment of the Punjab weakened the government in Lahore and delivered the various doabs into the hands of the Sikh" confederacy "called the misis" [text-layer?] [misls]; Bulleh Shah "was fated to see a second invasion of the Punjab by Ahmad Shah Abdali in 1748 and then a third in 1752," (p. 27, folio 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "1758", the year of Bulleh Shah's death, as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "As Bulleh Shah lay dying in 1758 at the age of 78, the rape of the Punjab was proceeding apace" (p. 28, folio 8).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the province"; refers to: "In the years to come" after Aurangzeb's period, with Banda Bairagi "(1670-1716)", Abdul Samad Khan, Zakriya Khan, Nadir Shah and "Shahidganj in 1746" — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "In the years to come, when plunder became routine in the Punjab, Bulleh Shah had no choice of factions. The Sikh marauders under Banda Bairagi (1670-1716) were to lay waste the whole of the province excluding Lahore and Qasur, imposing bone-crushing taxation .on [text-layer?] [on] the common peasant for the raising of armies on both sides" (pp. 42–43, folios 22–23).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — the passage runs from "The ancient cities of the Indus" to the present; author's argument, Introduction): "In terms of written sources, the Punjab and indeed the whole of the Indus basin, has always" seemed "a wasteland to scholars" (pp. 46–47, folios 26–27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — said of the survival of Baba Farid's and Shah Husain's poetry through Sikh collectors, the latter "in the 1940’s"; author's argument, Introduction): "A lack of intellectual tradition among the Muslims of the Punjab accounts for this phillistine [text-layer?] [philistine] indifference to the classics. Bulleh Shah was treated no differently" (p. 47, folio 27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after Dr. Nazeer Ahmad's selection "In 1976", i.e. the present of the Introduction; author's argument, Introduction): "After this the Punjab awaits a proper" scholarly "assessment of its greatest poet, a tryst that may never take place given the regional antecedents in the realm of letters" (p. 49, folio 29).
- (book's word: "Punjabi literature in Pakistan", "the Punjabis"; refers to: the present of the Introduction, date not stated in the passage; author's argument, Introduction — language-centred, kept because it states a condition of the Punjabis in Pakistan in general): "Punjabi literature in Pakistan is in a state of neglect. The language has become a purely spoken medium and the Punjabis are no longer able to" decipher "it in the written form" (p. 50, folio 30).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi"; refers to: "Viewpoint weekly (July 23, 1981)", present day; quoted source — the columnist "Janus", "‘Are you a Punjabi?’", a satirical questionnaire "in which ‘c’ stands for the Punjabi response", which Notes on the Introduction note 4 says "has accurately enumerated the ingredients of this lapse from character"): item (5), "You have lived in the Punjab all your life, but suddenly you come to know that your ancestors had come here from Central Asia", with the "Punjabi response" "(c) What the hell! I am a Bokhari and superior to all the low stock inhabiting the Punjab"; item (10), "All regional ‘languages [text-layer?] [languages] in Pakistan are taught in schools except Punjabi. All regional languages in Pakistan possess dictionaries except Punjabi"; item (11), "All regional personalities are moulded by a deep love of their idiom. A Sindhi will read Sindhi poetry and can tell you who is the greatest living Sindhi poet; so will the Pathan and the Baluch, Except [text-layer?] [Baluch. Except], of course, the Punjabi" (p. 236, folio 216; pp. 237–238, folios 217–218).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period as the verse gives it, "This century"; translation of the poet's verse — the poem "COME SEE ME ONCE IN A WHILE"; flagged: a verse line, kept only because it says something about the region and the Translator's Note (p. 16) reads it as political): "Now hell has opened its bogs. / Punjab has gone to the dogs. / This hell is the nethermost hell." … "This century cramps our style." (p. 149, folio 129).

## From Waris Shah, Hir and Ranjha (Usborne tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/waris_shah_hir_ranjha.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book names him in six forms and never once as "Fariduddin Ganjshakar" in full: **"Pir Chishti
Shakarganj"**, **"Shakarganj"**, **"The Shakarganj of Pakpattan"**, **"the holy saint of
Pakpattan"**, **"Farid Shakarganj of Pakpattan"**, **"Fariduddin Shakarganj"**, and — in the
annotator's voice only — **"Baba Farid"**.

- **Saint**
  - *The identification that secures the mapping, given flatly by the annotator on the book's last
    page.* Note 7: "**Shakarganj is the saint of Pakpattan.** See notes on Introduction." (p. 57,
    folio 56).
  - *The order, stated twice.* "**The Shakarganj of Pakpattan mentioned here belonged to The
    Chishtiyya Sect.**" (p. 2, folio 1, note 2); and "**The Chishtiyya sect of Sufis, which counts
    Fariduddin Shakarganj among its leaders**, regards music (Sama) as a permissible means of
    experiencing spiritual exaltation." (p. 19, folio 18, note 1 to Ch. 12) — attached to Ranjha's
    playing the flute before the Five Pirs, and the only silsila named in that whole chunk.
  - *Pakpattan as home and burial place — the annotator's, not the poem's.* Note 4: "Much as one
    would like to agree with Waris Shah this is not historically correct. The Punjab has had plenty
    of troubles **even after Baba Farid honoured it by making it his home and his place of burial!**"
    (p. 2, folio 1). **This is the only statement of a burial place for any archive figure in the
    Invocation chapter.** The identification Shakarganj = Baba Farid is made here by the annotator,
    not by the poem.
  - **No death date, no custodian, no ʿurs month, no shrine fabric and no building date appear
    anywhere in the book** — the row's d. 1266 is (not stated).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - *A shrine fair, its pilgrims and the physical act, in one sentence used as a measure of crowd
    size.* "The crowd and the noise was as great as at the **Fairs of Pakpattan** or Nigah or Rattan
    or Thamman, **where women flock to kiss the tomb of the saint and attain the achievement of their
    desires**." (p. 24, folio 23). Three usable facts: Pakpattan holds a Fair; **women** are the
    actors named; the act is **kissing the tomb**, and its purpose is "to attain the achievement of
    their desires". No date, month, duration, custodian, offering or fee — all (not stated). The
    sentence never names the saint; the mapping is by place. See Doubts.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - *His arrival delivers the Punjab — and the annotator denies it in the same breath.* Waris Shah's
    own Invocation: "Praise also to the beloved Pir Chishti Shakarganj. **When Shakarganj made his
    abode at Pakpattan, the Punjab was delivered of all its troubles.4**" (p. 2, folio 1); against
    note 4, quoted above.
  - *One of the Five Pirs who meet Ranjha, named second of the five.* "Good fortune however came to
    him and he met the Five `Pirs'` [text-layer?] [Pirs¹] on the way. First came Khwaja Khizr, lord of
    all the waters, **then Shakarganj, the holy saint of Pakpattan**." (p. 10, folio 9).
  - *He gives Ranjha a kerchief, and the relic is later used.* "The Five Pirs were gracious unto
    Ranjha, Khizr gave him a turban tuft,' Sayyid Jalal a dagger, `Zalkaria` [text-layer?] [Zakaria]
    a thick blanket, Lal Shahbaz a ring, **and Shakarganj a kerchief**. And they said, 'No one will do
    you harm. God has made you the owner of these cattle.'" (p. 11, folio 10); and at the crisis, "at
    midnight he remembered the Five Pirs. **He kissed the handkerchief of Shakarganj** and took the
    ring of Lal Shahbaz" (p. 50, folio 49).
  - *He is named as the Five Pirs' first-ranked "spiritual celebrity".* "the Five Pirs number among
    themselves such spiritual celebrities, as Khwaja Khizr, **Farid Shakarganj of Pakpattan**,
    Shahbaz Qalandar of Sind, Baha-ud-Din Zakaria and Makhdum `Jalianian` [text-layer?] [Jahanian] of
    Multan." (p. 16, folio 15, note 10).
- **Significance**
  - *Credited by Waris Shah with the poem itself.* "**With the aid of Shakarganj7 I have conferred
    this benefit on the world.**" (p. 57, folio 56).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` at p. 24, folio 23 rests on the place alone.** "the Fairs of
  Pakpattan… the tomb of the saint" — **the sentence never names the saint**. The mapping is secured
  only by the last page's note 7, "Shakarganj is the saint of Pakpattan." (p. 57, folio 56), plus the
  Invocation's "The Shakarganj of Pakpattan" (p. 2, folio 1). Note 7 itself defers — "See notes on
  Introduction." — to a note **that is not in the 57-page PDF**. Confirm before the fair material
  enters the row.

- **`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`, `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya`, `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: no
  death year for any of them.** The rows carry 1266, 1267 and 1274; the book gives no date for any
  saint it names. **There is no death year, no AH date and no era date anywhere in the book for any
  archive figure** — the only dates in the whole volume are 1739, 1748, 1766, `i 768`, `i1761`, 1809,
  "1180 Hijri", "the year 120 of the Bikramajit era", the damaged Karbala pair `6' AN.` / `68o`, and
  the reign lengths "28 years" and "700 years".

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan", period not stated; translation of the poet's verse, the Invocation — FLAG: verse): "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan, the Punjab was delivered of all its troubles.4"; the annotator's note 4 rebuts it: "Much as one would like to agree with Waris Shah this is not historically correct. The Punjab has had plenty of troubles even after Baba Farid honoured it by making it his home and his place of burial!" (p. 2, folio 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Ranjha is still a well-known caste of the Jats in the old Punjab." (p. 3, folio 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Mauj Din is now a common name In the old Punjab." (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "the Punjab village"; refers to: past, "were", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Spinning parties — the Punjabi name is ‘tarinjan’ or ‘bhora’ — were a famous institution of the Punjab village. Young girls, generally unmarried ones, sat together wit [text-layer?] [with] their spinning-wheels and competed with each other in spinning skill and scandalous conversation." (p. 4, folio 3, note 14).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Lassi is a favourite drink in the Punjab countryside." (note 6); "Washing the hair with curds is still well known in the Punjab countryside." (note 8) (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "a Punjab village"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "This is how a morning begins even now in a Punjab village." — glossing the verse's dawn at the mosque (p. 5, folio 4, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Khojas are a well-known tribe of Hindu converts to Islam in the old Punjab who are mainly engaged in trade." (p. 7, folio 6, note 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna" and "1748" as printed; annotator's note, historical): "The Punjab has often been the battleground of invading forces from the North. The campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna are famous. Here, the poet may be thinking of Ahead [text-layer?] [Ahmad] Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. and who, in 1748, began a series of invasions of India through the Punjab." (p. 8, folio 7, note 8); a later note on the verse "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" says "This is probably a reference to the invasion of Ahmad Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. See Chapter 4, note 8." (p. 48, folio 47, note 5).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the past", and "the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809" as printed; annotator's note, historical): glossing the verse "as if the armies of the Punjab had fallen upon Hindustan.2" (p. 7, folio 6): "Historically speaking, this is an imaginary situation, as in the past the armies of the Punjab are known to have defended Hindustan on more than one occasion rather than to have fallen upon it at any time. Even the Sikh power which rose long after Waris Shah’s poem was written, was contained on the banks of the Sutlej river by the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809, , and never became a menace to Hindustan. The poet’s flight of fancy may have been inspired by a mistaken sense of local patriotism." (p. 8, folio 7, note 2). The book marks "the Sikh power" as a unit of its own; no boundary is inferred.
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; translation of the poet's verse — FLAG: verse similes, nothing said about the region beyond invasion as a figure of speech): "as if armies from Kandahar had swept over the Punjab.8" (p. 7, folio 6); "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" (p. 47, folio 46); "looted him as armies have looted the Punjab3" (p. 44, folio 43 — the note answering marker 3 was not identified).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "There are also other ‘Bars’ in the old Punjab, such as ‘Nili Bar’ in the Montgomery area (now renamed Sahiwal). ‘Bar’ may he [text-layer?] [be] described as a jungle." (p. 10, folio 9, note 3; the note's first sentence, on Sandal Bar "between the Jhelum and Chenab flyers" [text-layer?] [rivers], is one territory and is not carried).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the more fashionable Punjabi turban"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century" as printed, and present tense; annotator's note, historical): the turban tuft "is the well-known ‘turrah’, beloved of the rural aristocracy of the Punjab. It spreads like a peacock’s tail over the side or the back of the more fashionable Punjabi turban. The tufted turban is a comparatively recent development, and i5 [text-layer?] [is] said to have been invented by the ‘mirasis’ or hereditary bards of the Punjab in the late nineteenth century." (p. 11, folio 10, note 7).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab area"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): bhang "is a favourite with the mystically inclined, particularly among Muslims and Sikhs. It is still smoked, chewed, eaten or drunk in the old Punjab area by wandering mendicants and many of those seeking spiritual exaltation or sexual pleasure" (p. 12, folio 11, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical/literary): "Sohni-Mahiwal has its setting In The old Punjab." — Sohni "belonged to Gujurar [text-layer?] [Gujrat], on the river Chenab"; "SoJtni-Mahiwal [text-layer?] [Sohni-Mahiwal] and Sossi-Funoo [text-layer?] [Sassi-Punnoo] have also travelled beyond their original setting, being popular both in the old Punjab and Sind." (p. 12, folio 11, note 5). Also under `sindh`.
- (book's word: "the Punjab villages"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): of a mother's threat, "A grossly exaggerated way of asserting parental authority. The expression is by no means unfamiliar in the Punjab villages even now." (p. 14, folio 13, note 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Fingers in their mouths is a typical female gesture common in the old Punjab." (p. 14, folio 13, note 10).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: past, "had", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Old houses in the Punjab countryside had a big courtyard with a number of living-rooms and bedrooms round it. A number of brothers living with their parents as a joint family was quite common." (p. 16, folio 15, note 7).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: past, "used to be", with "In our times" contrast; annotator's note, historical): "A spinning-wheel (charkha) used to be the inseparable companion of every woman, old or young, in the rural Punjab. Young girls and brides had red spinning-wheel, of smaller size. The spinning-wheel was an important link in rural society. Women spun cotton yarn, and the village weaver made it into cloth." (p. 17, folio 16, note 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab", "Punjab towns"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "The Gujars are a famous tribe of the old Punjab who keep animals and sell their milk. They also engage in agriculture on a large scale. A number of Punjab towns, such as Gujranwala, Gujrat and Gujar Khan are named after the Gujar tribe." (p. 19, folio 18, note 3).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, "was regarded", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Judging by their names, some of Hirs [text-layer?] [Hir's] girl friends are Hindus. This symbolises the atmosphere of the village in the old Punjab, where the village community as a whole was regarded as one social unit irrespective of caste or creed."; a girl "marr~1g [text-layer?] [marrying] into that village would be its daughter-in-law’", and "Even a sweeper woman would be addressed as daughter’, sister’ or ‘mother’" (p. 20, folio 19, note 12).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present tense, "has always been", period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present — FLAG: about one tribe, framed against the region): "the Sial tribe has always been more than usually considerate towards its women. It is one of the few tribes in the Punjab which allows women to inherit property under customary law." (p. 20, folio 19, note 13).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, "were often worn", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "A favorite game with young girls in the old Punjab. Printed scarfs, with a red background and white flowers, were often worn on the head by young girls who, when playing together, threw these scarfs at each other." (p. 21, folio 20, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, "is alive"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): of a ceremonial dish, "A more common dish for such purposes is rice cooked in ghee and well sprinkled with sugar. The institution is alive in the countryside of the old Punjab" (p. 23, folio 22, note 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "It may appear strange that a Muslim family should depend on Hindu Brahmans for fixing a marriage but this was by no means unusual in the old Punjab." (p. 26, folio 25, note 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Sawan and Bhadon are the two rainy months of the year in the old Punjab." (p. 26, folio 25, note 2).
- (book's word: "the old Panjab"; refers to: present tense, "now a valuable export from Pakistan"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): Basmutti rice "is grown In the central districts of the old Panjab. By all accounts it is the best in the subcontinent and is now a valuable export from Pakistan." (p. 26, folio 25, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab", "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: past, with "still" contrast; annotator's note, historical and ethnographic present): the Arsi "was one of the favourite ornaments of a bride in the old Punjab, and continued to be worn for a couple of months or so after marriage, during which period the newly-married girl was not expected to do household work. This is still the case in the less sophisticated parts of the Punjab countryside." (p. 26, folio 25, note 7).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, with "The social pattern is now changed"; annotator's note, historical): "Brides in the old Punjab wore coloured (and colourful) garments. White clothes, by tradition, were worn by widows, particularly among the Hindus, No ornaments were worn." Muslim widows "if they were childless, wore black or white"; "The social pattern is now changed." (p. 28, folio 27, note 4).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "A traditional Punjab marriage still retains the symbolism of ‘marriage by capture’." — the bridegroom "complete with horse and dagger, is made to look like a general"; "Resistance from the brides side now takes the form of abusive language towards the bridegroom and his family" (p. 28, folio 27, note 5).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "According to popular superstition in the Punjab countryside Pin [text-layer?] [Pirs], Fakirs and Jogis can, with, the force of their piety, make the Moving Finger of Destiny cancel what it has written." (p. 31, folio 30, note 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Gur is raw sugar. It is well known as a ceremonial offering in the Punjab countryside. An invitation to a wedding or other social occasion accompanied by gun [text-layer?] [gur] cannot be trifled with." (p. 33, folio 32, note 3).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note): "‘King’ and Nad’ are both musical instruments, popular in the old Punjab." — the printed instrument name `‘King’` is an unread form under the book's damage class 13 (p. 39, folio 38, note 2).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: "even today"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "In similar circumstances one would expect to hear similar compliments in the Punjab countryside even today!" (p. 39, folio 38, note 10).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "A snub nose is not regarded as a point of beauty either in men or women in the old Punjab." (p. 39, folio 38, note 14).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Jugglers and their bears are still a familiar sight in the old Punjab. Some have monkeys which are equally popular." (p. 40, folio 39, note 16).
- (book's word: "Punjabi mothers"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present — FLAG: an adjective of people, kept because it states a practice): "Lighting lamps in mosque even with ordinary oil is still a well-known way of expression of gratitude to God for His favours. Punjabi mothers still promise lamp-lighting in mosques when they pray for their children," (p. 41, folio 40, note 5).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): the ‘Mehndhi’ is "a fringe of delicately intertwined strands of hair all round the forehead of a virgin girl in the old Punjab. The ‘Mehndhi’, which is a symbol of virginity, disappears at the wedding and is not resumed after marriage." (p. 43, folio 42, note 2).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): millet grain "is even now considered inferior food in the Punjab countryside." (p. 44, folio 43, note 7).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: "is, or used to be"; annotator's note): "‘Dandasa’ is, or used to be, the equivalent of lipstick in the rural Punjab. It is made from the bark of certain trees which, when rubbed on the teeth and lips, gives them a gentle red colour." (p. 48, folio 47, note 2).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "While boys and girls in the rural Punjab move about freely to do their daily work and can see or talk to each other, it is not usual for them to do so if they are betrothed to be married. After the ‘mangni’ or betrothal, a girl does not see her fiancé until marriage" (p. 48, folio 47, note 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "Even now", against an earlier period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "The use of soap for shaving is a much later invention in the Punjab. Even now, shaving with water without soap is common with village barbers."; "barbers, even in the Punjab, do not shave beards without water." (p. 55, folio 54, note 2).
- (book's word: "Punjabi homes"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note — FLAG: adjective, kept because it states a domestic usage): the ‘pearha’ is "an article of furniture common in Punjabi homes." (p. 56, folio 55, note 5).
- (book's word: "the wretched Punjab"; refers to: the poet's own day as the Epilogue frames it — the poet dates the poem "in the year 1180 Hijri" on the same page; translation of the poet's verse, Epilogue, which Usborne says is "translated at full length" — FLAG: verse; the line turns at once to one town): "Of all the wretched Punjab I am most concerned for Kasur." (p. 56, folio 55). The preceding lament speaks of "the country" ("The country and the people have all been reduced to rump [text-layer?] [ruin?]. There is great disturbance throughout the country"), and the annotator's note 1 on it says "The latter half of the eighteenth century, to which Waris Shah is referring here, marked the decline of the Mughal Empire in India, and was a period of great confusion and disturbance" (p. 57, folio 56) — that note names India and the Mughal Empire, not Punjab.

## From Madho Lal Hussein, Verses of a Lowly Fakir (Alam tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/madho_lal_hussein_verses_lowly_fakir.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: present day, "more than 400 years after his death", the book giving "Shah Hussein (1538–99)"; translator's argument): "Weaver, mystic, fakir, Shah Hussein continues to command great reverence in Punjab as a poet–saint more than 400 years after his death" (p. 6, folio 6).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "Shah Hussein’s times", the reign of Akbar — no year given; translator's historical argument): "there’s the relevant historical account of a rebellion brewing in Punjab led by Abdullah Bhatti, commonly known as ‘Dullah Bhatti, who was captured and hanged in public on Akbar’s orders" (the leading turned comma in "‘Dullah" is printed, not damage, per the takeaways Doubts) (p. 14, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: Shah Hussein's time as dramatised; quoted source — Najm Hosain Syed's play *Takht L’hore (Throne of Lahore)*, a dramatic reconstruction, as reported by the translator): "Najm Hosain Syed extrapolates the socio-economic conditions of Punjab, not different from the ones cited in Moreland’s quote above" — Moreland's quoted account (W.H. Moreland, on "the so-called Golden Age of the Mughal rule") speaks of "India taken as a unit", not of Punjab, and is not itself a Punjab statement (p. 14, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Punjabis"; refers to: present day, "Even to this day"; translator's argument — FLAG: a people-word, and the book does not say which side of any border; kept because it describes the region's population): "Kafis, the stanzaic poems couched in folkloric themes and techniques, were easy to understand and memorize, and thus served as the ideal means to disseminate the mystical teachings among the illiterate masses dependent on the oral transmission of knowledge. Even to this day, most Punjabis and Sindhis are more likely to have heard a kafi than read one" (p. 15, folio 15).

## From Scott Kugle, Sufis and Saints' Bodies

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kugle_sufis_and_saints_bodies.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**The second row of this book, and it is a Chishti-lineage portrait rather than a shrine portrait.**
Kugle names him `Shaykh Farid al-Din "Ganj-i Shakar"`, "Farid al-Din", "the shaykh", and once, in a
quoted oath, "Farid al-Din [Ganj-i Shakar] Ajhodani" — the square brackets Kugle's own (p. 159, folio
138). The index files him as `Farld al-Dln AjhodanI[text-layer?] [Farīd al-Dīn Ajhodanī],
"Ganj-i Shakar," 138, 238-40, 243, 310 (n. 30)` (p. 353, folio 332), the quotation marks around the
sobriquet as printed. **He is not filed under `Baba Farid`, `Ganj Shakar`, `Ganjshakar` or
`Pakpattan`, none of which is a headword: (not stated).** The archive row's principal figure is "Baba
Fariduddin Mas'ud Ganjshakar"; **Kugle never calls him Masʿud anywhere.**

- **Saint**
  - **Rank in the Chishti community.** "the third axial saint after Shaykh `Mucin[text-layer?]`
    [Muʿin] al-Din Chishti" (p. 259, folio 238). **The book does not say who the first and second
    are: (not stated).**
  - **A death year that does not match the archive.** "Upon his death in 1265, Farid al-Din sent his
    staff, sandals, and other tokens of his authority to Nizam al-Din in Delhi." (p. 260, folio 239).
    The digits `1265` print clean. **The archive row records `figure_died` 1266.** Both stand; the
    sentence is also internally odd as printed, since the sending is placed "upon his death" — see
    Doubts.
  - **Place of death, given once, in a note about somebody else.** "Shaykh Sabir's cave retreat is
    preserved near the place where `Farld al-Dln`[text-layer?] [Farid al-Din] died (in Pakpattan in
    Pakistan); his tomb is in the town of Kalyar, north of Delhi, where he lived." (p. 331, folio 310,
    ch. 5 n. 30). **"his tomb" in that sentence is Sabir's, not Farid's** — the note's subject is
    Sabir and Farid appears as the geographical anchor. **This is the only occurrence of "Pakpattan"
    in the book.** No death date, no ʿurs, no custodian, no shrine description: all **(not stated)**.
  - **His settlement pattern, stated as a contrast with the rest of the order.** "Shaykh Farid al-Din
    lived far from the major urban areas where other Chishti masters were increasingly settling. In
    `Punjabi[text-layer?]` [Punjabi — printed clean; retained as printed] jungles, he pursued
    strenuous devotional exercises." (pp. 259–260, folios 238–239; the sentence straddles the page
    break). **Chunk 015's range names no town, no district and no shrine site for him:
    (not stated).**
  - **A retreat in the Punjab, with Yogi visitors.** "There are elliptical textual references to
    discussions between Shaykh Farid al-Din and some Hindu Yogis who visited his retreat in the
    Punjab" (p. 264, folio 243). Kugle immediately qualifies the evidence as "these tantalizing
    fragments of evidence" and notes that "the intriguing history of Sufi interactions with Yogis has
    received very little scholarly attention" (p. 264, folio 243). **The book names no text in which
    these references occur: (not stated).**
  - **He is the only South Asian saint in the ascension oath Khidr teaches Muhammad Ghawth** — "and by
    the Intimacy of Farid al-Din [Ganj-i Shakar] Ajhodani, and by the Renunciation of the Mahdi of the
    end of time" (p. 159, folio 138). The quality attached to him is **"Intimacy"**, one term in a
    graded series that also assigns "the Spirit of Jesus", "the Witness of Zakaria", "the Speech of
    Moses", "the radiance of the advent of the Light of Muhammad, the beloved of God", "the Closeness
    of Ali, the friend of God", "the Beauty of Dhu al-Nun Misri", "the Obedience of Junayd
    `Baghdad![text-layer?]` [Baghdadi]" and "the Insight of Bayazid Bistami" (p. 159, folio 138). He is
    also **the only figure in the list placed by a nisba rather than by a city named outright** —
    "Ajhodani". **The chunk does not gloss "Ajhodani", does not mention Pakpattan or Ajodhan, and does
    not say where he is buried in that range: all (not stated).** Kugle offers no comment on him in
    his own voice anywhere in pp. 146–161.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **His signature practice, named and described.** "He perpetuated in South Asia the Central Asian
    Sufi practice of the 'inverted prayer' (`salat-i maqusl)[text-layer?]` [salat-i maqus) — the
    terminal `l` appears inserted; **no confident reading offered**]. This consisted of a spiritual
    retreat for prayer by hanging by one's feet from a rafter or in a dry well, remaining in an
    inverted state of contemplation from daybreak to sunset, often continued as an isolated retreat
    (`chillaj[text-layer?]` [chilla)] for forty days in succession." (p. 260, folio 239).
  - **It was not imposed on followers.** "Though Farid al-Din never made this arduous exercise
    obligatory for his followers, it was integral to his own spiritual practice, and he saw it as
    crucial to forcefully dispel selfishness through such muscular ascetic practices." (p. 260,
    folio 239).
  - **Succession, and the material tokens of office.** "Near the end of his life, Shaykh Farid al-Din
    delegated his authority to a young follower, Nizam al-Din `Awliya3[text-layer?]` [Awliyāʾ]. Farid
    al-Din charged him to become the embodiment of love, through whom the comforting shade of divine
    mercy would pour forth in the capital city of Delhi, infusing all corners of the Islamic empire in
    India." (p. 260, folio 239) — delegation in life, confirmed by physical transfer of "his staff,
    sandals, and other tokens of his authority" to Delhi in 1265 (p. 260, folio 239).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **His spiritual heat.** "With them, he unleashed an inner spiritual heat and force that could
    either propel or singe others, depending upon the sincerity of their approach toward him."
    (p. 260, folio 239). Attributed by Kugle to Farid al-Din's ascetic practice, **without a source
    named in the sentence itself**.
  - **The refusal of office to Shaykh Sabir, on the ground of excessive power.** "His ability to
    channel this force into performing miracles was so audacious that Shaykh Farid al-Din was wary of
    granting him leadership of the community and exposing the wider public to his spiritual heat.
    Instead, he sent the young Shaykh Sabir out to the wilderness" (p. 260, folio 239). Sabir's power
    is likened to "the Yogic concept of tapas, the inner force awakened by devotional penance"
    (p. 260, folio 239). The counter-claim is recorded as partisan: "Followers of Nizam al-Din insist
    that Shaykh Sabir was passed over for succession in the Sufi order precisely because of his
    spiritual power." (p. 260, folio 239).

- **Significance**
  - **He is the point at which the Chishti order forks, and the fork is a succession dispute over his
    authority.** The Sabiris "take their name from a mysterious Sufi named `Ala3[text-layer?]` [ʿAlāʾ]
    al-Din `All[text-layer?]` [Ali] Sabir. He was a follower and nephew of Shaykh Farid al-Din
    'Ganj-i Shakar'" (p. 259, folio 238); "not all Chishtis were followers of Nizam al-Din, even if the
    majority were, for Nizam al-Din was not the only follower of the passionate and ascetic Shaykh
    Farid al-Din. There was also the shaykh's close follower and nephew, Shaykh Sabir, who continued
    his `masters[text-layer?]` [master's] more reclusive and athletic style of devotion" (p. 260, folio
    239). Sabir's followers "regarded him as the true inheritor of Shaykh Farid al-Din's saintly
    authority and the only one of his followers to adhere closely to the saint's rigorous concentration
    on bodily control", while "respecting Nizam al-Din and his amazing popularity they insisted that
    Shaykh Sabir was an equally authentic branch of the Chishti masters" (p. 261, folio 240).
    **Reported by Kugle as the self-understanding of the Sabiris, not as his own finding.**
  - **The Sabiri initiatic chain runs past him, not through him.** "It is to `Mucin[text-layer?]`
    [Muʿin] al-Din that the Sabiri branch of the Chishti community, to which Imdadullah belonged,
    traces its lineage of spiritual initiation." (p. 258, folio 237). **The book gives Farid al-Din no
    silsila of his own, no pir and no khalifa list beyond Nizam al-Din and Sabir: (not stated).** A
    later chain also bypasses him: Sabir's line "claimed a conventional initiation from Jalal
    `PanipatI[text-layer?]` [Panipati] via Shams al-Dln Turk PanipatI from Shaykh Sabir." (p. 332,
    folio 311, ch. 5 n. 31).
  - **The index puts him with the Chishti material and nowhere else.** His locators `238-40` and the
    note `310 (n. 30)` are shared with `Sabir, Ala5[text-layer?] [Alāʾ] al-Dln All[text-layer?]
    ['Ali], 238-40, 310 (n. 30), 311 (n. 31)` (p. 362, folio 341); `Chishti Sufi way … 236-52`
    (p. 351, folio 330). **No tomb, no ʿurs, no custodian and no date in the index: all
    (not stated).**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`salat-i maqusl)`** (p. 260, folio 239) — the terminal `l` appears inserted; no confident reading
  of the transliteration is offered, though the practice it names is described in full.

- **`1265`** — Farid al-Din's death year (p. 260, folio 239). Digits print clean, **but the archive
  row records 1266 and the sentence that carries the year is internally odd as printed** (the sending
  of the tokens is placed "upon his death"). A human must decide whether the book means "at his
  death" or "before his death" and whether 1265 or 1266 stands.

- **Farid al-Din's death year.** Book **1265** (p. 260, folio 239) against archive **1266**.

### Place-level material: Pakpattan

*The book's place-level material on "Pakpattan" (1 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

  (the book's "Pakpattan"): **named once, and the single row it takes is the one that already
  has its own subsection above** — "Shaykh Sabir's cave retreat is preserved near the place
  where `Farld al-Dln`[text-layer?] [Farid al-Din] died (in Pakpattan in Pakistan)" (p. 331,
  folio 310, ch. 5 n. 30). The material is recorded in full under
  `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`; the place-level attachment adds no second row. `Pakpattan`
  is **not** an index headword: **(not stated)**.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the medieval centuries", relative to Kabir "a century earlier" — as the book states, no year given; author's argument, in an endnote): "New Muslims (naw-musalman) from weaver castes converted to join the Islamic community in Punjab slightly later than in Hindustan, where the mystic poet Kabir displays the same kind of Sufi-bhakti synthesis a century earlier. The castes of weavers who converted to Islam in the medieval centuries had most likely been Shudras" (ch. 4 n. 52, printed on p. 329, folio 308).
