#### For the record — a shrine-id collision and a mis-filed bullet

**'Ala' ad-Din of Pakpattan is not the archive's `shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari`.** The 'Ala'
ad-Din in this material is a diwan of *this* shrine — Baba Farid's grandson, died 1335, whose tomb
at Pakpattan carries the inscription Safar 737 / Sept.–Oct. 1336 and is "the most imposing
structure in the entire shrine complex" (p. 221 and n. 19). The archive's
`shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari` is, per the chunk 018 Doubts row, "Hazrat Meeran Muhammad Shah
Bukhari (Mauj Darya) of Edward Road, Anarkali, Lahore, **died 1604** — a different man, city,
century and tomb." That row concludes: "**All the material here belongs to
shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar.** Recorded as a flagged non-match, consistent with chunk 015's
note."

**Action required:** at least one chunk's bullet was filed under the Lahore id
(`shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari`) by mistake and **needs re-filing to
`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`**. The chunk 018 note flags it and says chunk 015 flagged the
same non-match. The re-filing itself is not done in this part file — the mis-filed bullet is not
among this CSV's 105 `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` rows, so it must be located in the
`shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari` group file or in `chunk_015.notes.md` / `chunk_018.notes.md` and
moved there. *The epithet "Mauj Darya" for the Pakpattan 'Ala' ad-Din is not attested anywhere in
this CSV; it comes from the coordinator's brief for this pass and is recorded here as the reason
for the collision, not as a reading of Eaton.*

*Minor archive-shrine subsections (shrines group 2) and the book's cross-cutting material.
Page references are PDF page indices. Everything below comes from the chunk notes in
`out/ocr/eaton_essays_islam_indian_history/chunks/`; nothing is added from outside them.
Reviewed: no.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the present-day religious map; author's argument): "a glance at a map of South Asia's religious demography shows that Islam took root much more readily in Bengal or the Punjab than in upper India, the area with the deepest traditions of Indo-Muslim rule and patronage" (p. 14, folio 2).
- (book's word: "the western Punjab", "rural Punjab"; refers to: "between the thirteenth and nineteenth centuries", and the twentieth-century devotees of the 1938 case; author's argument, introduction summarising Chapters Nine and Ten): Chapter Nine "seeks connections between the processes of Islamization and peasantization in the western Punjab", asking "why and how it was that Jat tribesmen, as they abandoned pastoral nomadism for settled agriculture between the thirteenth and nineteenth centuries, gradually took on an Islamic identity" (p. 16, folio 4); Chapter Ten asks how the shrine "served to promote a conceptual conflation of Heaven and Earth among Muslims of rural Punjab" (p. 17, folio 5).
- (book's word: "Punjab and Sind"; refers to: "Beginning in the fourteenth century and continuing through the Mughal period (1526-1858)"; author's argument): "in both wings of India that became Muslim-majority regions—Bengal in the east, Punjab and Sind in the west—the growth of Muslim societies correlated with the adoption of sedentary agriculture. And both regions were still frontier societies where Hindu religious values and the hierarchic social ideals of Brahmin priests had not yet deeply penetrated"; "Thus in Punjab and Sind, shrines of great Sufis attracted and integrated pre-agrarian and non-Muslim pastoral clans into their ritual, socio-economic, and political orbits"; Sufi descendants married into the clan leadership, "the Delhi sultans and Mughal emperors granted huge tracts of rich land" for the shrines' ritual ceremonies, and the communities were "passing from a life of pastoral nomadism to one of settled wheat agriculture", helped by irrigation that "permitted a rapid growth in wheat production" (pp. 48–49, folios 36–37).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "by the eighteenth century"; author's argument): "by the eighteenth century large communities of Muslim peasants had appeared in both Punjab and Bengal. In contrast, in India's heartland … conversion to Islam was far less significant"; "Unlike Punjab or Bengal, Malabar lay beyond the orbit of Mughal imperial influence and the Persianized culture associated with it" (p. 49, folio 37).
- (book's word: "northwest Punjab"; refers to: 986; book's historical prose): "The pattern commenced in 986, when the Ghaznavid Sultan Sabuktigin (r. 977-97) attacked and defeated the Hindu Shahi raja who controlled the region between Kabul and northwest Punjab" (p. 110, folio 98).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "From the mid-eleventh century"; book's historical prose): Mahmud's successors "became progressively more provincial, their kingdom focused around their capital of Ghazni in eastern Afghanistan with extensions into the Punjab"; their raids "appear to have been less destructive and more sporadic than those of Sabuktigin and Mahmud" (p. 111, folio 99).
- (book's word: "Punjabi saints", "the Western Punjab"; refers to: 1911, which the author says is "equally true of medieval shrines"; quoted source, Major Aubrey O'Brien, "The Mohammedan Saints of the Western Punjab", 1911, in a footnote): "The general idea of our riverain folk seems to be that the Deity is a busy person, and that his hall of audience is of limited capacity … Thus, all human beings require an intervener between them and God" (p. 216, folio 204).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the late tenth century onward", culminating "in the thirteenth century"; book's historical prose): Mongol pressures "forced waves of Turkish settlers into the subcontinent, many of them settling permanently in the urban centres of the Punjab, such as Lahore, Dipalpur, and Multan"; Baba Farid's settling at Ajudhan was "the religious dimension of a very slow transformation of the Punjab's cities from a Hindu to a Turkish-Islamic orientation" (p. 217, folio 205).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "from the thirteenth century to the present", the essay's present; ethnographic present): writing ta'widh (amulets) for the masses "has not changed from the thirteenth century to the present; even now the successors of Baba Farid and his assistants, in common with those of other shrines in the Punjab, continue to write ta'widh for the masses" (p. 218, folio 206).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the mid-thirteenth century", Balban; book's historical prose): Balban, "in order to build a defensive bulwark against Mongol incursions, pursued the policy of strengthening and populating certain cities in the Punjab with large garrisons of Turkish elements", which "augment[ed] considerably the pattern of Muslim settlement in the urban centres of the Punjab, including not only the soldiers but also thousands of refugees fleeing before the advancing Mongols—artisans, merchants, petty officials, and the like"; the footnote names the garrisoned cities as "Lahore, Multan, Sirhind, Bhatinda, and Dipalpur" (pp. 222–223, folios 210–211).
- (book's word: "the western Punjab"; refers to: period of the essay's medieval argument, with "always" for the rainfall; book's historical prose, quoting Emily Hodges' dissertation in progress): the urban centres of the western Punjab were "economically based on intensive cultivation of food crops along the flood plains of the rivers"; beyond them "lay a vast tract of sparsely populated land, the barr country between the five rivers", "but little cultivated owing to the very scanty rainfall that has always-characterized [text-layer?] [always characterized] the western Punjab", supporting "pastoral nomadism based primarily on the herding of goats and camels"; the pastoralists "'moved only down to the riverain [sic] in the hot dry months and returned to the barr and ¢ha/ [text-layer?] [thal?] after rains, never leaving the Punjab plains and covering at the most a distance of less than one hundred miles'", in "a symbiotic relationship with the settled peoples of the riverine area" (p. 223, folio 211).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the eleventh century to Babur's "1519 and again in 1525"; book's historical prose): the Jat pastoralists, "before their entry into the Punjab, had not yet been integrated into Hindu society"; "By the time of Baba Farid, however, in the 1260s, we find the first mention of Jats occupying the Punjab proper" (the doabs named are listed under not attached); Babur "described Jat pastoralist groups in the Sind Sagar doab and Sialkot regions"; "The northward movement of Jat clans and their settlement in the grazing tracts of the Punjab is thus well supported by contemporary evidence" (pp. 224–225, folios 212–213).
- (book's word: "the Lahore and Multan sibas" [text-layer?] [subas], "Punjabi parganas", "the Punjab"; refers to: statistics "compiled about 1595", Abu al-Fazl's day, "by the 1650s", and "Babur's time"; book's historical prose, citing Abu al-Fazl, Ibbetson and Rose, and Irfan Habib): the sentence opens on damaged text, printed "i Akban ’statistics for the Lahore and Multan sibas, compiled about 1595" [text-layer?] [opening words lost]; counting castes named as Jat, "of the total 186 Punjabi parganas whose dominant zamindirs [text-layer?] [zamindars] are known, fully 82, or nearly half, were controlled by Jat groups"; "Only in the extreme western Punjab, in the Sind Sagar doab, where the Janjua Rajputs and Ghakkars dominated, were the Jats conspicuously absent as zamindars"; the Jats, the "'low Sudras' described by al-Birtni [text-layer?] [al-Biruni] in the eleventh century", had become "the dominant agrarian caste in nearly half of the Punjab"; "by the 1650s, as today, the very word 'Jat' had become virtually synonymous with peasant agriculturalist"; Habib's explanation is "the introduction of the Persian wheel in the Punjab and its extensive use by Babur's time", making "much of the arid western Punjab's naturally rich soil capable of supporting the cultivation of food crops". The scholar's name is printed "Abi al-Fadl" and "Abii al-Fadi" [text-layer?] [Abu al-Fadl] (p. 225, folio 213).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "Punjabi Muslim clans", "the rivers of the Punjab"; refers to: "the medieval period"; author's argument): the Jats' "migration north from Sind to Punjab, their rise in social status from low Sudras to zamindars, and their gradual transformation from pastoralists to farmers" was accompanied by conversion to Islam; "of those Jat and Rajput groups that became Muslim in the medieval period, the vast majority of them claim to have been converted either by Baba Farid or by his contemporary, Baha' al-Haqq Zakariya (d. 1263), whose tomb is in Multan city"; Table 9.1 lists "some of the Punjabi Muslim clans" that claim Baba Farid (Bhatti, Chhina, Dhudhi, Dogar, Gondal, Hans, Jo'iya, Khokhar, Siyal, Tiwana, Watt, Kharral, Ara'in); the earliest sources on Baba Farid "make no mention of his having converted anyone", and many of these clans, "in the course of their northward migrations up the rivers of the Punjab, had not yet reached the Ajudhan area during Baba Farid's lifetime" (p. 227, folio 215).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "during Jahangir's reign"; book's historical prose): the Jawahir-i Farid account was "written during Jahangir's reign, when the Mughals enjoyed effective authority in the Punjab"; "In the mid-eighteenth century, however, Mughal decline had allowed various local powers to assert their independence" (the decline sentence does not name the Punjab) (p. 231, folio 219).
- (book's word: "Punjabi clans"; refers to: "from the late fourteenth to the early nineteenth centuries", the span of the Siyal data; author's argument): from the Siyal genealogies of Jhang District (Table 9.2), "If these data on the Siyals are at all indicative of the conversion pattern for the other clans … then we may conclude that religious conversion among Punjabi clans was very slow indeed—not only slow, but probably unconscious as well", "a period long before either British census officials or zealous reformers began urging Indians to place themselves into sharply differentiated religious categories" (pp. 234–235, folios 222–223).
- (book's word: "the southwestern Punjab"; refers to: the 1935–38 court depositions; quoted source, the Lahore High Court record): "many of the major endogamous clans that are spread throughout the southwestern Punjab, mainly as peasant agriculturalists" claimed conversion by Baba Farid; "These clans, or binidaris [text-layer?] [biradaris] (brotherhoods), include the Wattu, the Siyal, the Dogar, the Kathia, the Bhatti, and the Tiwana" (p. 241, folio 229).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Mughal officials newly arrived in Bengal, around 1620 in the surrounding narrative; book's historical prose): "The Bengali diet of fish and rice, for example, contrasted sharply with the wheat and meat diet of the Punjab and seems to have posed a special stumbling block for immigrants" (p. 263, folio 251).

## From Carl W. Ernst and Bruce B. Lawrence, Sufi Martyrs of Love

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/ernst_lawrence_sufi_martyrs_of_love.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's densest archive row by a wide margin: 105 bullets across sixteen of the seventeen chunks.
The book's names for him are **"Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar"** (running form), **"Shaykh Farid"**,
**"Shaykh Farid ad-Din"**, **"Baba Farid"**, **"Farid ad-Din Mas'ud Ajodhani [Ganj-i Shakar]"**
(square brackets the book's own, p. 159, folio 147), **"Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakar"** in the Persian
bibliography (p. 236, folio 226), **"Shakarganj"** and **"Shakarbar"** in nineteenth-century titles
(p. 223, folio 212), once **"Ganj-i Shakkar"** (p. 12, folio not stated; p. 236, folio 226) and once
**`Ganj-i Shakzar[text-layer?]` [Ganj-i Shakar]** (p. 237, folio 227). His personal name is given as
**"Mas'ud"**, and only inside the starving-son anecdote (p. 81, folio 67; p. 168, folio 156).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year — the book prints 1265 six times and never 1266.** "Shaykh Farid (d. 1265)"
    (p. 17, folio 2); `19.   Farid ad-Din Ganji I Shakkar (d. 1265)` [`Ganj-i Shakkar`] in the
    table `lNITIATIC GENEALOGY` [`INITIATIC GENEALOGY`] `OF THE EARLY CHISHTIS` (p. 12, folio not
    stated); "19. Farid ad-Din `Mas'udAjodhani[text-layer?]` [Mas'ud Ajodhani] Ganj-i Shakar
    (d. 1265)" (p. 77, folio 63); "the tomb of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265) at Ajodhan
    (modern Pakpattan)" (p. 105, folio 92); "Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265)" (p. 111,
    folio 98); and the bibliography heading `E. Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265)` (p. 223,
    folio 212). **The archive row's `figure_died` reads 1266 — see Doubts; no reconciliation was
    made.**
  - **Two Hijri death years, printed twelve pages apart and irreconcilable as printed.** The
    appendix's ʿurs calendar gives "Muharram / 5. Farid ad-Din Mas'ud Ajodhani [Ganj-i Shakar]
    (d. 664/1265)" (p. 159, folio 147); the translated death notice gives "The Shaykh died on 5
    Muharram 634 A.H. at the venerable age of 95" (p. 169, folio 157). **634 and 664 are both
    printed; no Common Era equivalent is given at p. 169, and the book joins the two nowhere.**
    The age at death, 95, is given only at p. 169, folio 157.
  - **Where he is placed in the lineage.** No. 19 of the initiatic genealogy, between
    `18.   Qutb ad-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki (d. 1235)` and `20.   Nizam ad-Din Awliya'` (p. 12, folio not
    stated); "the nineteenth generation" (p. 77, folio 63); "Shaykh Farid was in the first
    generation of Indian-born masters of the Chishti order" (p. 111, folio 98); third of "the five
    human pillars on whom the Indian extension of the Chishti order was built", after Mu'in ad-Din
    Ajmeri and Qutb ad-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki and before Nizam ad-Din Awliya' and Nasir ad-Din
    `Chiragh-i-Dihli-these[text-layer?]` [Chiragh-i Dihli—these] (p. 80, folio 66); one of "the
    three Chishti giants, Farid ad-Din, Nizam ad-Din, and Nasir ad-Din, who complete the first cycle
    of the Chishtiyya" (p. 183, folio 171); and head of the appendix's second generation, "The first
    three generations are headed by Mu'in ad-Din, Farid ad-Din, and Nasir ad-Din" (p. 161, folio
    149) — **note that Qutb ad-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki is absent from that list of three although his
    biography stands between theirs** (p. 161, folio 149).
  - **The lineage claim that matters most to the archive: "Both the major sublineages of the
    Chishtis derive from him"** (p. 167, folio 155), and "The centuries-old division between the two
    branches stems from the time of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar, and it is his two disciples 'Ala'
    ad-Din 'Ali Sabir and Nizam ad-Din Awliya', after whom these two branches are named" (p. 131,
    folio 118). The Sabir filiation is reported as contested, not as fact: Shaykh 'Ali ibn Ahmad
    Sabir "is said to have been the Shaykh 'Ali Sabir who is briefly mentioned in Siyar al-awliya'
    as a disciple of Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar. No less an authority than Shaykh 'Abd
    al-Haqq, however, questions the conflation of the two names and persons" (p. 74, folio 60).
  - **Initiation, dated only by an event in Multan.** "The visit of Qutb ad-Din to Multan (a
    strategic commercial center in the Punjab) became the occasion for Farid ad- Din's pledge of
    allegiance to the Chishti master" (p. 80, folio 66) — the printed form is "Farid ad- Din" with
    an intruded space. **No year is given for the visit: (not stated).**
  - **Family.** "Each of the first three Indian Chishti `masters-Mu'in[text-layer?]`
    [masters—Mu'in] ad-Din, Qutb ad-Din, and Farid `ad-Din-had[text-layer?]` [ad-Din—had] families"
    (p. 81, folio 67); "it is Farid ad-Din who poignantly attests to the tension peculiar to Muslim
    saints, the tension between bearing responsibility for one's family and abandoning all to God"
    (p. 81, folio 67). **Numbers of wives and children: (not stated)**, though "one of his wives",
    "a son of ours" and "one of Farid ad-Din's sons" (pp. 81, 87, folios 67, 73) show there was more
    than one of each. His mother is named: "Bibi Sharifa became a widow at an early age and had no
    children besides Farid ad-Din. Until her dying breath, she continued to remember God, and was
    considered to be a great perfected saint" (p. 135, folio 122) — Thanvi's account; **Bibi
    Sharifa is given no dates, no burial place and no shrine: (not stated)**. His daughter Bibi
    Fatimah is "a prominent daughter of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar" through whose elder son Khwaja
    Muhammad Imam the Faridi blood line runs (p. 126, folio 113). The descendants are a named
    kin-group: "the descendants of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar (the Faridis) have shaped the
    continuation of Chishti identity" (p. 126, folio 113). Shaykh Salim Chishti of Fatehpur Sikri
    belongs to "the Faridi line" (p. 111, folio 98).
  - **Sayyid status left unresolved by the book itself.** He falls inside "All but one were
    sayyids, i.e., they traced their biological ancestry back to the Prophet Muhammad", who
    "preferred to be addressed by the title \"shaykh\" rather than \"sayyid`;'\"[text-layer?]`
    [\"sayyid.\"]" (p. 80, folio 66). **The book does not say which one of the five is the
    exception: (not stated).** He also falls inside "All received a protracted, formal education",
    "They either memorized the Qur'an or knew many of its passages by heart", and a "thorough
    grounding in Muslim law (`jiqh[text-layer?]` [fiqh])" (p. 80, folio 66).
  - **Austerity, quoted twice from two different sources.** "His reputation for vigils, fasting,
    and penances extended back to his boyhood when he used to be regarded as \"crazy\" for the
    severity of his observances. Customarily he broke his fast by eating stale bread" (p. 81,
    folio 67). In the appendix: he broke his fast with "a cup of sherbet to which a small quantity
    of grapes had been added", gave away half or two-thirds, then "he was served two pieces of bread
    with oil", and "the Shaykh would not eat again till the next day at iftar"; for sleep "he used
    the rug upon which he had been sitting during the day as his bed, even though the rug did not
    cover his feet" (p. 167, folio 155). Nizam ad-Din "noted that Shaykh Farid usually ate stale
    bread at the time of iftar", Nasir ad-Din that "For years they would bring stale bread to Shaykh
    Farid ad-Din", and Nizam ad-Din that "That night when I ate delah or the fruit of the karil tree
    was like a festival to me; it was a gift of grace" (p. 167, folio 155). Scruple about borrowed
    salt: "Some flavoring was used in this `food;'[text-layer?]` [food,\"] he commented, \"and it is
    not fitting that I should eat such `food:'\"[text-layer?]` [food.\"] (p. 167, folio 155).
  - **He never performed the hajj, and the book supplies the excuse.** "While Mu'in ad-Din had
    undertaken the pilgrimage before coming to India, Qutb ad-Din, Farid ad-Din, Nizam ad-Din, and
    Nasir ad-Din all failed to fulfill this critical obligation of Islam. It must be quickly noted,
    however, that they espoused poverty and, therefore, were not technically able to go on the
    pilgrimage" (p. 81, folio 67).
  - **His death, narrated.** After evening prayers in congregation he fell unconscious, asked on
    waking whether he had prayed, said them a second and then a third time — "\"I had better say
    them one more `time;'[text-layer?]` [time,\"] he said: \"Who knows what may happen?\"" — and
    "At the end, he exclaimed \"`0[text-layer?]` [O] Living one, `0[text-layer?]` [O] Eternal one!\"
    and gave up his soul to God" (p. 169, folio 157).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The tomb is at Pakpattan, and the book names the town seven times.** "pilgrims at the shrine
    of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in Pakpattan (Pakistani Punjab)" (p. 17, folio 2); "Farid ad-Din
    in Pakpattan", the third station of a nineteenth-century meditation circuit after Qutb ad-Din
    in Delhi and Mu'in ad-Din in Ajmer, walked by Khayrabadi (p. 71, folio 57); "One had to go to
    the desert to reach the tomb of Farid ad-Din at Pakpattan/Ajodhan, or that of Hamid ad-Din at
    Nagaur in Rajasthan" (p. 99, folio 86); "the tomb of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265) at
    Ajodhan (modern Pakpattan)" (p. 105, folio 92); "Ajmer became one of the main points on a
    pilgrimage itinerary, along with Delhi and Pakpattan" (p. 98, folio not stated); Hasan Nizami's
    "trip on foot (partially) to Pakpattan in emulation of the saint's trip to find his master
    Farid" (p. 127, folio 114); and the index's `Pakpattan 2, 5, 92, 103, 114` (p. 249, folio 240).
    **The book gives no description of the building, no founding or construction date, no
    dimensions and no plan anywhere: all (not stated).**
  - **Custodians at Pakpattan, with their competing titles, in one sentence** — "Examples of shrines
    in Pakistan comparable to Ajmer include Baba Farid's tomb and its custodians
    (`diwans!gaddi nishins!sajjada nishins[text-layer?]` [diwans/gaddi nishins/sajjada nishins]) at
    Pakpattan … both discussed at length by David Gilmartin" (p. 116, folio 103). **No custodian is
    named, and no succession list is given: (not stated).**
  - **Ajodhan is where the book says he settled, and the book never says Ajodhan is Pakpattan in its
    own voice.** "After wandering from city to city, he at last settled in Ajodhan. At that time its
    inhabitants were harsh-mannered people who worshiped the world and had no faith in dervishes. He
    said when he arrived there: \"This is the place where I will `settle:'\"[text-layer?]` [settle.\"]"
    (p. 167, folio 155). The only equation the book prints is the parenthesis "Ajodhan (modern
    Pakpattan)" (p. 105, folio 92) and the compound "Pakpattan/Ajodhan" (p. 99, folio 86); the
    appendix and chapter 13 write "Ajodhan" alone (pp. 167, 177, folios 155, 165). **See Doubts.**
  - **The hospice and the meditation site at Ajodhan.** "Outside the town there was a grove of karil
    trees. One was especially thick, and Farid ad-Din used to spend his time meditating under that
    tree, though he also frequented the `con-gregational mosque`. It was there in Ajodhan that his
    children were born. Often they suffered pangs of starvation and underwent hardships" (p. 167,
    folio 155). The "Ajodhan hospice" is named as the place Nizam ad-Din was driven from (p. 87,
    folio 73), and a classroom stood "under the minaret (of the mosque in Ajodhan)" where "a certain
    scholar by the name of Ziya ad-Din" held classes (p. 169, folio 157).
  - **Two chilla-gahs of his outside Pakistan** — "one chillah of Baba Farid abuts the shrine of
    Khwaja Mu'in ad-Din in Ajmer, while another associated with Farid is found outside the tomb of
    Qutb ad-Din in Delhi" (p. 109, folio 96). **No Pakistani chilla-gah is attached to him:
    (not stated).**
  - **A relic of his is held at Khuldabad, not at Pakpattan** — a staff, "which is called the staff
    `ofFarid[text-layer?]` [of Farid] ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar", cited from `Ahsan al-aqwal`, chapter 7,
    p. 55, and from `Ghara'ib al-karamatwa 'aja'ib al-mukashqfot` (Persian MS, `Khu1dabad[text-layer?]`
    [Khuldabad], Fariduddin Saleem), p. 27 (p. 218, folio 207). **The book does not say what country
    or province Khuldabad is in: (not stated).** A second set of relics of his was buried with his
    successor: "When Nizam ad-Din was laid in his grave, they covered his body with the cloak that
    he had received from Farid ad-Din and they put under his blessed head the prayer mat of the
    Shaykh" (p. 176, folio 164).
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The ʿurs, described as a multi-day festival with cowries thrown over the crowd.** "Today, at
    the multi-day annual festival celebrating his 'marriage' with God, cowrie shells (once used as
    the smallest units of currency) are thrown over the crowd, to imitate the moment when Shaykh
    Farid ad-Din went into ecstasy, on realizing that Nizam ad-Din Awliya' had just attained a
    status rare even among the great ones, the nearly unprecedented state of becoming the beloved of
    God Himself" (p. 18, folio 3). **The book names no month, no date, no custodian and no crowd
    figure in this passage: (not stated).**
  - **The ʿurs date is given elsewhere, and it heads the appendix's Chishti calendar** —
    "Muharram / 5. Farid ad-Din Mas'ud Ajodhani [Ganj-i Shakar] (d. 664/1265)" (p. 159, folio 147),
    i.e. the fifth of Muharram, which is also the day of death given at p. 169, folio 157.
  - **State control of the ʿurs — the book's single statement of a Pakistani government managing a
    named shrine's festival.** "Government manipulation, as in Pakistan's control of the 'urs of
    Baba Farid, may present an institutional, ideological face to this practice that many
    \"pure-minded\" mystics would resist or resent." (p. 157, folio 145). The sentence continues
    into a limit on that control: "Still, no state-controlled `apparatus—whether` in Pakistan,
    India, or Bangladesh, Saudi Arabia or Iran, or the United `States—can` have ultimate control."
    (p. 157, folio 145). **No Auqaf department, no ministry, no date, no revenue figure, no visitor
    number and not even the town of Pakpattan is named in this passage: all (not stated).**
  - **A named pilgrimage to the tomb, dated by a named traveller.** "The famous Moroccan traveler
    Ibn Battuta made such a pilgrimage to the tomb of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265) at
    Ajodhan (modern Pakpattan) around the year 1340" (p. 105, folio 92) — the earliest date the
    book gives for the tomb functioning as a pilgrimage destination.
  - **The 1930s Pakpattan education dispute — the book's most concrete colonial-succession
    episode.** "a debate raged in Chishti circles in the 1930s over whether or not the British could
    compel a minor who had become diwan at Pakpattan to obtain education at a British college in
    Lahore" (p. 116, folio 103). The protest is quoted: "All the sajjada nishins from other major
    Chishti shrines in North India protested 'that the sajjada nishin should be a Darwesh and has no
    need of receiving the education which is imparted to the sons of rich families.'" (p. 116,
    folio 103). **The book does not name the minor diwan, the college, or the outcome:
    (not stated).**
  - **Zikr formulas in Indian languages are traced to him.** "Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar is the
    source of specific zikr formulas, including some in Indian languages" (p. 45, folio 31);
    Awrangabadi "cites zikr formulas in Punjabi and Hindi, such as the following, attributed to the
    famous Chishti saint Farid ad-Din: 'Say *wuhi hi* upwards, *hi hi* to the left side of the
    breast, *hin hi* toward the heart.'" (p. 47, folio 33). Their circulation and background:
    "Variations on this Hindi `zikrofFarid[text-layer?]` [zikr of Farid] ad-Din are well known in a
    number `oflndian[text-layer?]` [of Indian] Sufi texts, particularly among Chishti and Shattari
    masters. These phrases belong to a long tradition of adapting `hath a yoga[text-layer?]` [hatha
    yoga] mantras to Islamic themes and Sufi practices, as illustrated in the many translations of
    the curious yogic text known as *The Pool of Nectar*" (p. 47, folio 33).
  - **Samaʿ — he is listed among the Indian shaykhs who favoured it, and his own ruling is
    quoted.** Ashraf Jahangir Simnani "extends the list of bygone saints who favored music to
    include Indian shaykhs, viz., Qutb ad-Din, Qazi Hamid ad-Din, Farid ad-Din, and, of course,
    Nizam ad-Din", and "describes these saints not merely as advocates of `sama'but[text-layer?]`
    [samaʿ but] as those who 'experienced empathetic ecstasy and danced'" (p. 57, folio 43). Asked
    "about the permissibility or illegality of sama', a topic disputed among the 'ulama", he
    replied "\"Praise be to God!\"… \"One group has been burnt to ashes, while the other is still
    arguing.\"" (p. 170, folio 158).
  - **Learning versus devotion — his ruling, and the authors' warning about it.** Nizam ad-Din
    asked "What is your command? I will abandon scholarship and pursue my devotions and
    supererogatory `prayers:'\"[text-layer?]` [prayers.\"]"; "Shaykh Farid ad-Din replied: \"I do
    `notre-strain[text-layer?]` [not restrain] anyone from learning. Do both till one predominates.
    A dervish should have some measure of `learning:'\"[text-layer?]` [learning.\"]" (p. 87,
    folio 73). **Ernst and Lawrence immediately caution: "This anecdote is not found in Fawa'id
    al-fu'ad. It may or may not be true" (p. 87, folio 73)** — the appendix gives the same exchange
    again, with Nizam ad-Din "twenty years old" (p. 171, folio 159).
  - **He is the precedent for the khilafat-nama as a document** — of Nizam ad-Din choosing his
    leading disciples from a list of 32 candidates, "He drew up their diplomas (khilafat-namas)
    according to a strict procedure that Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar employed" (p. 194, folio 182).
  - **The succession rule for women, quoted through Thanvi.** "Her son, Baba Farid, used to say,
    \"If *khilafat* and *sajjada* [deputyship and successorship] could be given to a woman, then
    undoubtedly she was worthy of getting the *khilafat*.\" See how precisely he followed the
    *shari'a*, observes Thanvi, alluding to the *sunna* that says that a woman can not be given the
    succession" (p. 135, folio 122). The book prints **"can not"** as two words; kept as printed.
  - **His malfuzat and who recorded them.** "Some of the sayings [malfuzat] of Shaykh Farid ad-Din
    Ganj-i Shakar have been recorded by Shaykh Nizam ad-Din Awliya in a letter" (p. 169, folio 157),
    with further dicta at p. 170, folio 158; in the Persian bibliography he is the subject of
    `Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakar. Rahat al-qulub. Comp. Nizam al-Din Awliya'. Monzavi, III, no. 2656
    (22 MSS); no. 3696 (3 MSS)` and the compiler of `Qutb al-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki (d. 633/1235).
    Fawa'id al-salikin. Comp. Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakar. Monzavi, III, no. 3421 (24 MSS).`
    (p. 238, folio 228).
  - **He is credited with no books, on his successor's testimony.** "I have not written any book
    because neither Shaykh `al-Islarn[text-layer?]` [al-Islam] Farid ad-Din nor Shaykh al-Islam Qutb
    ad-Din nor any of the Chishti saints before them wrote any book." (p. 178, folio 166). Against
    this, the bibliography lists two works "(attr.)" to him — `Ganj-i asrar. Monzavi, III, no. 3355
    (9 MSS).` and `Risala-i 'irfani. Monzavi, III, no. `27I4[text-layer?]` [2714] (2 MSS).` (p. 241,
    folio 231) — **"(attr.)" being the book's own flag of doubtful ascription** — and prayers
    "transmitted from Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakkar by his khalifa 'AbdAllah" in `Muhammad Lud'ha.
    Awrad-i ruhi. Monzavi, III, no. 2313.` (p. 245, folio 235).
- **Legends & Miracles (karamat)**
  - **The inverted chilla, attributed to him in three places and argued over in one.** "Stories of
    his rigid ascetic practice include the performance of the 40-day upside-down meditation"
    (p. 20, folio 5); "Perhaps most impressive is his narration of the performance of the inverted
    *chilla*, the upside-down meditation performed for 40 days. Baba Farid was reputed to have
    practiced this unusual form of meditation. How could this be justified? Thanvi concludes that if
    it is performed as a practice stipulated for drawing a person closer to God then it is not
    allowable, but if it is done in order to cure some moral defect, then it may be permitted"
    (p. 135, folio 122) — note **"was reputed to have practiced"**, not that he did. The appendix
    locates it: "Farid ad-Din performed the \"the inverted 40-day fast\" [chilla-i ma'kus] in the
    well of the Hajj mosque `ofUcch[text-layer?]` [of Ucch]. For 40 days they would suspend him from
    a branch of the tree that overhung the well every night. At daybreak they would pull him out"
    (p. 168, folio 156).
  - **The epithet "Ganj-i Shakar" explained twice, and the book says so.** The pebbles-to-sugar fast
    under Qutb ad-Din's direction ends "From that day forth he has been called Ganj-i Shakar, \"the
    Treasure of `Sugar:'\"[text-layer?]` [Sugar.\"]"; the book then names its source — "The previous
    passage is recorded in Siyar al-Awliya' in the section explaining how Farid ad-Din received the
    name Ganj-i Shakar. In addition to the above, there is another well-known story about how he
    came to have this epithet" — the sugar merchant whose load turned to salt and back (p. 168,
    folio 156).
  - **A karamat at prayer, narrated by Nizam ad-Din.** Introduced as "one of the most famous such
    incidents, of Farid ad- Din while at prayer" (p. 82, folio 68): prostrate and covered by his
    garment he describes an unseen visitor — "is he a Turk of medium size with slightly yellowish
    complexion?" … "Is he wearing a chain around his waist?" … "Has he anything in his ears?" —
    each detail confirmed, ending "Tell `him;'[text-layer?]` [him,\"] the shaykh told me, \"to go
    away before he is disgraced.\" The man took to his heels and disappeared" (p. 82, folio 68).
  - **The pomegranate seed, given as an allegation and left unexplained.** "once he is alleged to
    have consumed a single seed of a pomegranate brought by Jalal ad-Din Tabrizi, with astonishing
    results" (p. 81, folio 67). **The book does not say what the results were: (not stated).**
  - **The starving son, told twice, and Ernst and Lawrence's refusal to credit it.** "\"`0[text-layer?]`
    [O] Khwaja, today a son of ours is at the point of death from starvation.\" \"What can Mas'ud
    do?\" replied the shaykh, bowing his head. \"If it be the will of God and the boy departs from
    this world, tie a rope around his feet and throw him `outside:'\"[text-layer?]` [outside.\"]"
    (p. 81, folio 67); the appendix repeats it almost word for word (p. 168, folio 156). The
    authors: "The story is grim but probably exaggerated: Several of the saint's children did live
    to manhood and, in fact, were a credit to their ascetic father, whom they emulated in many
    respects" (p. 81, folio 67).
  - **A celestial vision of him, reported from a Shattari master.** In 'Abd ar-Rahman's "long entry
    on Farid ad-Din", "Muhammad Ghaws (d. 969/1562) … wrote about a dream in which he found himself
    in one of the highest celestial realms. There he came face-to-face with Farid ad-Din Ganj-i
    Shakar. Farid ad-Din explained that he shared that station with three other saints: Bayazid,
    Junayd, and `Zu'n-Nun`" (p. 75, folio 61).
  - **A posthumous appearance in a disciple's dream, disciplining hospitality.** "Shaykh Farid
    ad-Din appeared in a dream to Nizam ad-Din and said, \"You have remarked that even if there is
    nothing in the house, one is expected to show perfect courtesy to whomever comes. How then has
    it happened that someone has gone from your house with a heavy heart?\"" (p. 173, folio 161).
  - **Spiritual oversight sought across generations.** "At their last meeting, Burhan ad-Din Gharib
    requested from Nizam ad-Din Awliya' the gift of being under the direct spiritual observation
    (nazar) of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar" (p. 192, folio 180).
  - **A dream sign attributed to him in the twentieth century.** Hasan Nizami took Mihr 'Ali Shah as
    pir "believing he had a spiritual sign from Baba Farid" (p. 127, folio 114).
  - **A postmortem-miracle corpus exists and is catalogued** — `Qadi Shir Muhammad Pakpattani.
    Chishtiyya-i bihishtiyya. Monzavi, III, no. 2511 (2 MSS). On Farid's postmortem miracles,
    khalifas, and testimony of Baha' al-Din Zakariyya.` (p. 236, folio 226).
- **Significance**
  - **He is the archive-facing face of the book's transcreedal argument.** The order's audiences are
    listed in ascending distance beginning with "South Asian Muslim devotees, pilgrims at the shrine
    of Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in Pakpattan (Pakistani Punjab)" (pp. 17–18, folios 2–3); and "The
    same Shaykh Farid ad-Din is so highly respected by Sikhs that they have incorporated his Punjabi
    verses for their holy scripture, the Guru Granth Sahib" (p. 18, folio 3). He is also the root of
    the book's twentieth-century Western line: Hazrat Inayat Khan is "from the lineage of Farid
    ad-Din and Nizam ad-Din" (p. 18, folio 3).
  - **He is the book's extreme case of withdrawal from kings — and the case that broke.** "Perhaps
    no Chishti master was as renowned for his withdrawal from society at large and the company of
    kings in particular as Shaykh Farid ad-Din of Pakpattan… Yet even 'Shaykh Farid Ganj-i Shakar
    was forced by his circumstances to permit his disciples to circulate a begging bowl and collect
    food, [even though] no Chishti saint before him had permitted such a `practice:'[text-layer?]`
    [practice.\"]" (p. 20, folio 5 — the inner quotation is the authors' quotation of another
    writer, **unnamed in the chunk: (not stated)**).
  - **He refused land grants, and his family starved — the row's single most useful fact for the
    economics of this archive.** "Farid ad-Din later refused land grants that were offered to him,
    and routinely gave away all money and other gifts brought to his lodge. As a consequence, his
    family is said to have literally faced starvation on numerous occasions" (p. 81, folio 67).
    **Who offered the grants, when, and of what size: all (not stated).** He also refused to lobby a
    sultan: asked for a letter to "Sultan Ghiyas ad-Din Balban", he wrote "I entrust this man's fate
    to God and then to you. If you give him something, God is the giver but you will be thanked for
    it. If you give him nothing, God is the preventer, and you are `excused:'\"[text-layer?]`
    [excused.\"]" (p. 170, folio 158).
  - **Mughal legitimacy is anchored on him.** Akbar at Fatehpur Sikri drew "through him on the
    spiritual blessing that derived from his ascetic patron, Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in
    the Punjab" (p. 112, folio 99), so that "Through a twofold, redoubled Chishti loyalty, Akbar
    could anchor his imperial legitimacy in two provinces adjacent to Uttar Pradesh, the Punjab and
    Rajasthan" (pp. 112–113, folios 99–100). A Mughal grandee's devotion is recorded separately:
    "Though Khan-i Khanan Muhammad Bayram Khan enjoyed a high position in the Mughal court and had
    indescribable wealth and power at his command, nonetheless, with respect to the dervishes, he
    may be described as one who had faith in, and love of, them", with a four-line poem on Farid's
    name that he "is said to have composed" (p. 168, folio 156).
  - **The editors' own headnote.** He is "an extraordinary ascetic, noted for his controlled
    ecstasy; yet unlike Qutb ad-Din, he was also a loyal father and husband", "Reckoned among the
    greatest of the saints and the foremost of the Sufis, according to 'Abd al-Haqq" (p. 167,
    folio 155), and his life yields "the three characteristics of pious Chishtis: eschewing all
    stable income, engaging in meditation, often abetted by music, and training worthy disciples"
    (p. 167, folio 155). In the structural parallel: "The third Shaykh in each case (Farid ad-Din in
    the thirteenth century, Fakhr ad-Din in the eighteenth century) consolidates and extends the
    order" (p. 124, folio 111).
  - **The book states where its own life of him comes from** — "This anecdote and many other
    features of the life of `Shaykb[text-layer?]` [Shaykh] Farid ad-Din are provided in the
    Appendix, translated from the foremost biography of medieval Indian saints, Akhbar al-akhyar"
    (p. 207, folio 196).
  - **He carries the largest saint-section in the European-language bibliography** — twelve entries
    under `E. Farid al-Din Ganj-i Shakar` (p. 223, folio 212), against nine for Mu'in al-Din Chishti
    (p. 224, folio 213) and ten for Nizam al-Din Awliya' (pp. 224–225, folios 213–214) — plus
    `Farid al-Din Mas'ud "Gandj-i Shakar" (K. A. Nizami). `11:796-7.[text-layer?]` [II:796-7.]`
    (p. 231, folio 220). The index gives him `Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar 2, 3, 5, 13, 22, 33,43,60,61,67,68, 73,92,96,99, 113, 122, 145` (p. 247, folio not stated).
  - **Bibliography leads for this row, all page-referenced to where they are printed.** On the
    shrine itself: `Eaton, Richard M. "The Political and Religious Authority of the Shrine of Baba
    Farid in Pak-pattan, `Punjab:'[text-layer?]` [Punjab."] In Moral Conduct and Authority: the
    Place of Adab in South Asian Islam, edited by Barbara Metcalf. 333-356. Berkeley and Los
    Angeles: University of California Press, 1984.` and `Irving, M. "The Shrine of Baba Farid
    Shakarganj at `Pakpattan:'[text-layer?]` [Pakpattan."] Journal of the Panjab `Histori-cal
    Society![text-layer?]` [Historical Society 1] (1911-12): 70-76.` (both p. 227, folio 216); also
    `Chaghatai, Abdullah. "Baba Farid and `Pakpattan:'[text-layer?]` [Pakpattan."] Iqbal Review 9
    (1968): 116-31.` (p. 223, folio 212). On Punjabi and Sikh scripture: `Macauliffe, M. A. The Life
    of Shaikh Farid I, and the compositions of Shaikh Farid II, contained in the Granth Sahib of the
    Sikhs. Lahore: Artistic Printing Works, 1903.` (p. 223, folio 212); `Elahi, M., trans. Couplets
    of Baba Farid. Lahore: Majlis Shah Hussain, 1967.` (p. 229, folio 218); `Singh, Attar. "Sheikh
    Farid and the Punjabi Poetic `Tradition:'[text-layer?]` [Tradition."] In Perspectives on Sheikh
    Farid, edited by Gurbachan Singh Talib. Patiala: Baba Farid Memorial Society, 1975. 225-33.`
    (p. 230, folio 219) — **the book does not say that the Shaikh Farid of the Granth Sahib is the
    same man**; the entry is recorded as printed. The earliest European-language item listed:
    `Mohana Lala, Munshi. "A Brief Account of Mas'ud, known by the name of Shakarganj, or Shakarbar,
    the Muhammadan Saint." Journal of the Asiatic Society of Bengal 5 (1836): 635-38.` (p. 223,
    folio 212). The modern standard: `K. A. Nizami, The `Lift[text-layer?]` [Life] and Times
    `if[text-layer?]` [of] Shaikh Farid-u'd-Din Ganj-i-Shakar, `lAD[text-layer?]` [IAD]
    Religio-Philosophy Series No. 1 (Delhi: Idarah-i Adabiyat-i Delli, 1955; reprint ed., 1973)`
    (p. 213, folio 202; also pp. 215, 223, folios 204, 212). A Lahore imprint on him:
    `Wahid Bakhsh Sial, Maqam-i Ganj-i Shakkar (Lahore: Sufi Foundation, 1403/1983), p. 38`, cited
    in the notes to the ʿurs discussion (p. 210, folio 199; repeated p. 236, folio 226).
  - **Persian manuscript leads on him and his descendants** — `'Ali Asghar Chishti. Jawahir-i
    Faridi. Composed in 1133/1624, with emphasis on descendants of Ganj-i Shakar. Munzawi, XI, 888,
    no. 100 (3 MSS), citing also a Lahore, 1301 ed., and an Urdu translation.` (p. 234, folio 224 —
    **the AH and CE years do not agree; see Doubts**); `Gul Muhammad Shirawi [Ahmadpuri]. Gulzar-i
    Faridi. Biography composed in 1285/1868-69, then re-edited in 1300/1882-83. Munzawi, XI, 1052,
    no. 358 (3 MSS).` (p. 236, folio 226, square brackets the book's own); and, on a descendant,
    `Shah Bhakkari Burhanpuri. Hamid al-Din. Tazhkira-i Hadrat Shah Bhakkari Burhanpuri. … Although
    described as a malfuzat, it is a biography of a descendant of `Ganj-i Shakzar[text-layer?]`
    [Ganj-i Shakar], Nizam-al-Din Bhakkari (d. 897/`I492[text-layer?]` [1492]).` (p. 237, folio 227)
    — **the book's own genre correction is a source-critical judgement worth keeping.**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`** is substantive in most chunks, but in chunks 003 and 004 the
  mapping rests on a bare name — "the famous Chishti saint Farid ad-Din" (p. 47, folio 33) and a
  four-name list (p. 57, folio 43). Those two bullets should not be used as evidence about Pakpattan.

| Figure | Book's printing(s) | Archive row's `figure_died` | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar | `(d. 1265)` ×6, and `(d. 664/1265)` | 1266 | pp. 12, 17, 77, 105, 111, 159, 223 |

| Figure | Book's printing(s) | Archive row's `figure_died` | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar | `5 Muharram 634 A.H.`, "at the venerable age of 95" | — | p. 169, folio 157 |

**Nothing above has been reconciled.** The two Farid Hijri years — **634** (p. 169, folio 157) and
**664** (p. 159, folio 147) — are the sharpest internal contradiction in the book and a human must
check both printed pages before either enters an entry. The four Hujwiri years are printed in four
different parts of the book and joined nowhere.

The appendix and chapter 13 write **"Ajodhan"** and never write Pakpattan (pp. 167, 169, 177, folios
155, 157, 165); the body writes "Ajodhan (modern Pakpattan)" once (p. 105, folio 92) and
"Pakpattan/Ajodhan" once (p. 99, folio 86). **The archive row's location is Pakpattan. A human must
decide whether "the Ajodhan hospice" (p. 87, folio 73), the karil grove and the mosque-minaret
classroom (pp. 167, 169, folios 155, 157) may be treated as the predecessor institution of the
Pakpattan darbar. Until then those sentences should not enter an entry as statements about
Pakpattan.**

### Place-level material: Pakpattan

*The book's place-level material on "Pakpattan" (1 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** (the book's "Pakpattan"): **Pakpattan as a bare toponym on a fourteenth-century pilgrimage itinerary, and as an index line** — "In the century after Mu'in ad-Din's death, Ajmer became one of the main points on a pilgrimage itinerary, along with Delhi and Pakpattan" (p. 98, folio not stated), a route "followed in 1352 by Shaykh Zayn ad-Din Shirazi, the main successor to Burhan ad-Din Gharib in Khuldabad" (p. 98, folio not stated); and `Pakpattan 2, 5, 92, 103, 114` (p. 249, folio 240). Pakpattan holds exactly one archive row, which the person rule already gives this material, so the conversion adds nothing new.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — "the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular"; author's argument): "Not only for Gujarat, Bengal, but also for Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, and the Deccan does the significance of the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular, mark a particular kind of South Asian spiritual authority that is not only political and religious but also cultural"; the authors describe "a spectrum of spiritually charged spaces or sites" that are "devotional loci for Indian Muslims from several classes, and also for Hindus" (p. 110, folio 97). Punjab is one of five regions named together; nothing specific to Punjab is said.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", which the book calls one of "two provinces"; refers to: Akbar's reign, the Fatehpur Sikri phase, which he "abandoned ... by 1585" — as the book states; author's argument): through Shaykh Salim and "Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in the Punjab", "Through a twofold, redoubled Chishti loyalty, Akbar could anchor his imperial legitimacy in two provinces adjacent to Uttar Pradesh, the Punjab and Rajasthan, both of which also happened to be crucial to the political-military ambitions of his reign" (pp. 112–113, folios 99–100). The book's own unit is a Mughal-period "province"; its extent is not stated.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "The Nizami Chishtis came to prominence in the eighteenth century, principally in Delhi, the Deccan, and Punjab, through masters who were related to but in tension with local political leaders" (p. 119, folio 106).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi Hindus"; refers to: Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi's time, "as British colonialism began to take hold of India" — Taunsawi is "(d. 1850)" on the same page; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "urged his followers to participate in resistance against Sikh aggression in the Punjab, and so, apparently for the first time in the annals of Chishti history, we have spiritual biographies drawn into partisanship on behalf of one political group (Muslim) over against another (non-Muslim)"; "Notable, however, is the effort of Shah Sulayman to maintain cordial relations with Punjabi Hindus even while he opposed their Sikh neighbors" (p. 121, folio 108). The supporting endnote (n. 10) cites M. Zameeruddin Siddiqi, "The Resurgence of the Chishti Silsilah in the Punjab during the Eighteenth Century" (p. 214, folio 203).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the southern Punjab"; refers to: Taunsawi "(d. 1850)", his successor Muhammad 'Aqil "(d. 1814)" and the latter's disciple Gul Muhammad Ahmadpuri "(d. 1827)" — as printed; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "consolidated the order by stressing learning through establishment of institutions: not only the academy called Dar al- 'Ulum at Taunsa on the upper Indus, but also other religious schools throughout the Punjab"; 'Aqil and Ahmadpuri "both of whom founded religious schools in the southern Punjab that provided free instruction and lodging to hundreds of students" (pp. 121–122, folios 108–109).
- (book's word: "the upper Punjab"; refers to: "the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): the line from Shah Mirra "resurfaces in the nineteenth century in the figure of Hafiz Sayyid Muhammad 'Ali Khayrabadi, marking the expansion of the Nizami branch of Chishti Sufism through disciples of Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi in the upper Punjab" (p. 71, folio 57).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period"; author's argument): "If we were to follow only the dictates of political history and the criteria of social science, the test case for the Chishti Nizami Sulaymaniyya lineage in the colonial period might seem to be the battles over shrines in the Punjab"; the example given is Taunsa, and "Political power struggles internal to the Sulaymaniyya can be traced to British manipulation and also the rivalry of local Sikhs, Hindus, and Muslim reformers" (p. 124, folio printed "Ill" [text-layer?] [111]). The authors then set this political reading aside: "political contestation is only part of the narrative".
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present day ("continues to be intoned"); ethnographic present): "The name Qalandar continues to be intoned in popular qawwali lyrics of the Punjab, and it has been appropriated by a mainstream Sufi group, the Indian Qalandari order centered at Kakori near Lucknow" (p. 36, folio 22). Which side of the border is not stated.
